[P19-S00020] PDF pages [2]
Tool use refers to the ability of the model to recognize and execute function calls (e.g., to perform web search, complete a math problem, execute code).

[P19-S00021] PDF pages [2]
Information accurate as of publication date.

[P19-S00022] PDF pages [2]
In practice, this means that the 2.5 models are substantially better at providing safe responses without interfering with important use cases or lecturing end users.

[P19-S00023] PDF pages [2]
We also evaluated Gemini 2.5 Pro's Critical Capabilities, including CBRN, cybersecurity, machine learning R&D, and deceptive alignment.

[P19-S00024] PDF pages [2]
While Gemini 2.5 Pro showed a significant increase in some capabilities compared to previous Gemini models, it did not reach any of the Critical Capability Levels in any area.

[P19-S00025] PDF pages [2]
Our report is structured as follows: we begin by briefly describing advances we have made in model architecture, training and serving since the release of the Gemini 1.5 model.

[P19-S00026] PDF pages [2]
We then showcase the performance of the Gemini 2.5 models, including qualitative demonstrations of its abilities.

[P19-S00027] PDF pages [2]
We conclude by discussing the safety evaluations and implications of this model series.

[P19-S00028] PDF pages [2]
Sparse MoE models activate a subset of model parameters per input token by learning to dynamically route tokens to a subset of parameters (experts); this allows them to decouple total model capacity from computation and serving cost per token.

[P19-S00029] PDF pages [2]
Developments to the model architecture contribute to the significantly improved performance of Gemini 2.5 compared to Gemini 1.5 Pro (see Section 3).

[P19-S00030] PDF pages [2]
Despite their overwhelming success, large transformers and sparse MoE models are known to suffer from training instabilities (Chowdhery et al., 2022; Dehghani et al., 2023; Fedus et al., 2021; Lepikhin et al., 2020; Liu et al., 2020; Molybog et al., 2023; Wortsman et al., 2023; Zhai et al., 2023; Zhang et al., 2022).

[P19-S00031] PDF pages [2]
The Gemini 2.5 model series makes considerable progress in enhancing large-scale training stability, signal propagation and optimization dynamics, resulting in a considerable boost in performance straight out of pre-training compared to previous Gemini models.

[P19-S00032] PDF pages [3]
Gemini 2.5 Pro is a marked improvement over Gemini 1.5 Pro, and has an LMArena score that is over 120 points higher than Gemini 1.5 Pro.

[P19-S00033] PDF pages [3]
Cost is a weighted average of input and output tokens pricing per million tokens.

[P19-S00034] PDF pages [3]
Gemini 2.5 models build on the success of Gemini 1.5 in processing long-context queries, and incorporate new modeling advances allowing Gemini 2.5 Pro to surpass the performance of Gemini 1.5 Pro in processing long context input sequences of up to 1M tokens (see Table 3).

[P19-S00035] PDF pages [3]
Both Gemini 2.5 Pro and Gemini 2.5 Flash can process pieces of long-form text (such as the entirety of “Moby Dick” or “Don Quixote”), whole codebases, and long form audio and video data (see Appendix 8.5).

[P19-S00036] PDF pages [3]
Together with advancements in long-context abilities, architectural changes to Gemini 2.5 vision processing lead to a considerable improvement in image and video understanding capabilities, including being able to process 3-hour-long videos and the ability to convert demonstrative videos into interactive coding applications (see our recent blog post by Baddepudi et al., 2025).

[P19-S00037] PDF pages [3]
The smaller models in the Gemini 2.5 series - Flash size and below - use distillation (Anil et al., 2018; Hinton et al., 2015), as was done in the Gemini 1.5 series (Gemini Team, 2024).

[P19-S00038] PDF pages [3]
To reduce the cost associated with storing the teacher's next token prediction distribution, we approximate it using a k-sparse distribution over the vocabulary.

[P19-S00039] PDF pages [3]
While this still increases training data throughput and storage demands by a factor of k, we find this to be a worthwhile trade-off given the significant quality improvement distillation has on our smaller models, leading to high-quality models with a reduced serving cost (see Figure 2).

[P19-S00040] PDF pages [3]
Our pre-training dataset is a large-scale, diverse collection of data encompassing a wide range of domains and modalities, which includes publicly available web documents, code (various programming languages), images, audio (including speech and other audio types) and video, with a cutoff date of June 2024 for 2.0 and January 2025 for 2.5.

[P19-S00041] PDF pages [4]
Our post-training dataset, like Gemini 1.5, consists of instruction tuning data that is carefully collected and vetted.

[P19-S00042] PDF pages [4]
It is a collection of multimodal data with paired instructions and responses, in addition to human preference and tool-use data.

[P19-S00043] PDF pages [4]
This model family is the first to be trained on TPUv5p architecture.

[P19-S00044] PDF pages [4]
We employed synchronous data-parallel training to parallelise over multiple 8960-chip pods of Google's TPUv5p accelerators, distributed across multiple datacenters.

[P19-S00045] PDF pages [4]
The main advances in software pre-training infrastructure compared with Gemini 1.5 were related to elasticity and mitigation of SDC (Silent Data Corruption) errors: 1.

[P19-S00046] PDF pages [4]
Slice-Granularity Elasticity: Our system now automatically continues training with fewer “slices” of TPU chips when there is a localized failure, and this reconfiguration results in tens of seconds of lost training time per interruption, compared with the 10 or more minute delay waiting for healthy machines to be rescheduled without elasticity; the system continues training at around 97% throughput while the failed slice is recovering.

[P19-S00047] PDF pages [4]
At the scale of this training run we see interruptions from hardware failures multiple times per hour, but our fault tolerance machinery is designed to tolerate the higher failure rates expected at much larger scales.

[P19-S00048] PDF pages [4]
Split-Phase SDC Detection: On previous large-scale runs it could take many hours to detect and localize machines with SDC errors, requiring both downtime while debugging, and rollback/replay of a large number of potentially corrupt training steps.

[P19-S00049] PDF pages [4]
We now use lightweight deterministic replay to immediately repeat any step with suspicious metrics, and compare per-device intermediate checksums to localize the root cause of any data corruption.

[P19-S00050] PDF pages [4]
Empirically, accelerators that start to exhibit intermittent SDCs are identified within a few minutes, and quickly excluded from the job.

[P19-S00051] PDF pages [4]
During this run, around 0.25% of steps were replayed due to suspected SDCs and 6% of these replays turned out to be genuine hardware corruption.

[P19-S00052] PDF pages [4]
Both of the above techniques were relatively simple to implement due to the single-controller design of the Pathways system (Barham et al., 2022), which allows all accelerators to be coordinated from a single python program with a global view of the system state.

[P19-S00053] PDF pages [5]
Overall during the run, 93.4% of the time was spent performing TPU computations; the re-mainder was approximately spent half in elastic reconfigurations, and half in rare tail cases where elasticity failed.

[P19-S00054] PDF pages [5]
Around 4.5% of the computed steps were replays or rollbacks for model debugging interventions.

[P19-S00055] PDF pages [5]
Since the initial announcement of Gemini 1.5, significant advancements have been made in our post-training methodologies, driven by a consistent focus on data quality across the Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Reward Modeling (RM), and Reinforcement Learning (RL) stages.

[P19-S00056] PDF pages [5]
A key focus has been leveraging the model itself to assist in these processes, enabling more efficient and nuanced quality control.

[P19-S00057] PDF pages [5]
Furthermore, we have increased the training compute allocated to RL, allowing deeper exploration and refinement of model behaviors.

[P19-S00058] PDF pages [5]
This has been coupled with a focus on verifiable rewards and model-based generative rewards to provide more sophisticated and scalable feedback signals.

[P19-S00059] PDF pages [5]
Algorithmic changes to the RL process have also improved stability during longer training.

[P19-S00060] PDF pages [5]
These advancements have enabled Gemini 2.5 to learn from more diverse and complex RL environments, including those requiring multi-step actions and tool use.

[P19-S00061] PDF pages [5]
The combination of these improvements in data quality, increased compute, algorithmic enhancements, and expanded capabilities has contributed to across-the-board performance gains (as described in Section 3) , notably reflected in the significant increase in the model's LMArena Elo scores, with both Gemini 2.5 Flash and Pro gaining more than 110 points over their Gemini 1.5 counterparts (122 for Gemini 2.5 Pro and 111 for Gemini 2.5 Flash, see Figure 1), along with significant improvements on several other frontier benchmarks.

[P19-S00062] PDF pages [5]
Past Gemini models produce an answer immediately following a user query.

[P19-S00063] PDF pages [5]
This constrains the amount of inference-time compute (Thinking) that our models can spend reasoning over a problem.

[P19-S00064] PDF pages [5]
Gemini Thinking models are trained with Reinforcement Learning to use additional compute at inference time to arrive at more accurate answers.

[P19-S00065] PDF pages [6]
Our training recipe has evolved from the original experimental thinking model, Gemini 2.0 Flash Thinking (launched in December 2024), to the Gemini 2.5 Thinking series, which incorporates Thinking natively across all domains.

[P19-S00066] PDF pages [6]
The result is a single model that can achieve stronger reasoning performance across the board, and is able to scale up its performance further as a function of inference time (see Figure 3 for an example of the impact of Thinking).

[P19-S00067] PDF pages [6]
We integrated Thinking with other Gemini capabilities, including native multimodal inputs (images, text, video, audio) and long context (1M+ tokens).

[P19-S00068] PDF pages [6]
For any of these capabilities, the model decides for itself how long to think before providing an answer.

[P19-S00069] PDF pages [6]
We also provide the ability to set a Thinking budget, constraining the model to respond within a desired number of tokens.

[P19-S00070] PDF pages [6]
This allows users to trade off performance with cost.

[P19-S00071] PDF pages [6]
To demonstrate this capability, we conducted experiments where we systematically varied the thinking budget, measured in the number of tokens the model is allowed to use for internal computation.

[P19-S00072] PDF pages [6]
As shown in Figure 4, increasing this budget allows the model to scale its performance and achieve significantly higher accuracy.

[P19-S00073] PDF pages [6]
While most of the changes made to our training architecture and recipe since Gemini 1.5 have resulted in improvements across all capabilities, we have also made changes that have resulted in some capability-specific wins.

[P19-S00074] PDF pages [6]
We will now discuss these for code, factuality, long context, multilinguality, audio, video, and agentic use cases (with a particular focus on Gemini Deep Research).

[P19-S00075] PDF pages [6]
Gemini 2.0 and 2.5 represent a strategic shift of our development priorities towards delivering tangible real-world value, empowering users to address practical challenges and achieve development objectives within today's complex, multimodal software environments.

[P19-S00076] PDF pages [6]
To realize this, concerted efforts have been undertaken across both pre-training and post-training phases since Gemini 1.5.

[P19-S00077] PDF pages [6]
In pre-training, we intensified our focus on incorporating a greater volume and diversity of code data from both repository and web sources into the training mixture.

[P19-S00078] PDF pages [6]
This has rapidly expanded coverage and enabled the development of more compute-efficient models.

[P19-S00079] PDF pages [6]
Furthermore, we have substantially enhanced our suite of evaluation metrics for assessing code capabilities aligned with downstream use cases, alongside improving our ability to accurately predict model performance.

[P19-S00080] PDF pages [7]
During post-training, we developed novel training techniques incorporating reasoning capabilities and curated a diverse set of engineering tasks, with the aim to equip Gemini with effective problem-solving skills crucial for addressing modern engineering challenges.

[P19-S00081] PDF pages [7]
Key applications demonstrating these advancements include IDE functionalities, code agent use cases for complex, multi-step operations within full repositories, and multimodal, interactive scenarios such as end-to-end web and mobile application development.

[P19-S00082] PDF pages [7]
Collectively, these efforts have yielded broad and significant improvements in Gemini's coding capabilities.

[P19-S00083] PDF pages [7]
This progress is evidenced by superior performance on established benchmarks: performance on LiveCodeBench (Jain et al., 2024) increased from 30.5% for Gemini 1.5 Pro to 74.2% for Gemini 2.5 Pro, while that for Aider Polyglot (Gauthier, 2025) went from 16.9% to 82.2%.

[P19-S00084] PDF pages [7]
Furthermore, Gemini 2.5 Pro obtained an increase of over 500 Elo over Gemini 1.5 Pro on the LMArena WebDev Arena (Chiang et al., 2024; LMArena Team, 2025), resulting in meaningful enhancements in practical applications, including UI and web application development (Doshi, 2025a), and the creation of sophisticated agentic workflows (Kilpatrick, 2025).

[P19-S00085] PDF pages [7]
Within the context of generative models, ensuring the factuality of model responses to information- seeking prompts remains a core pillar of Gemini model development.

[P19-S00086] PDF pages [7]
With Gemini 1.5, our research was concentrated on enhancing the model's world knowledge and its ability to provide answers faithfully grounded in the context provided within the prompt.

[P19-S00087] PDF pages [7]
This effort culminated in the December 2024 release of FACTS Grounding (Jacovi et al., 2025), now an industry-standard benchmark for evaluating an LLM's capacity to generate responses grounded in user-provided documents.

[P19-S00088] PDF pages [7]
With Gemini 2.0 and 2.5, we have significantly expanded our scope to address multimodal inputs, long-context reasoning, and model-retrieved information.

[P19-S00089] PDF pages [7]
At the same time, the landscape and user expectations for factuality have evolved dramatically, shaped in part by Google's deployment of AI Overviews and AI Mode (Stein, 2025).

[P19-S00090] PDF pages [7]
To meet these demands, Gemini 2.0 marked a significant leap as our first model family trained to natively call tools like Google Search, enabling it to formulate precise queries and synthesize fresh information with sources.

[P19-S00091] PDF pages [7]
Building on this, Gemini 2.5 integrates advanced reasoning, allowing it to interleave these search capabilities with internal thought processes to answer complex, multi-hop queries and execute long-horizon tasks.

[P19-S00092] PDF pages [7]
The model has learned to use search and other tools, reason about the outputs, and issue additional, detailed follow-up queries to expand the information available to it and to verify the factual accuracy of the response.

[P19-S00093] PDF pages [7]
Our latest models now power the experiences of over 1.5B monthly active users in Google's AI Overviews and 400M users in the Gemini App.

[P19-S00094] PDF pages [7]
These models exhibit state-of-the-art performance across a suite of factuality benchmarks, including SimpleQA for parametric knowledge (Wei et al., 2024), FACTS Grounding for faithfulness to provided documents (Jacovi et al., 2024, 2025), and the Vectara Hallucination Leaderboard (Hughes et al., 2023), cementing Gemini as the model of choice for information-seeking demands.

[P19-S00095] PDF pages [7]
Modeling and data advances helped us improve the quality of our models' responses to queries utilizing our one million-length context window, and we reworked our internal evaluations to be more challenging to help steer our modeling research.

[P19-S00096] PDF pages [7]
When hill-climbing, we targeted challenging retrieval tasks (like LOFT of Lee et al., 2024), long-context reasoning tasks (like MRCR-V2 of Vodrahalli et al., 2024), and multimodal tasks (like VideoMME of Fu et al., 2025).

[P19-S00097] PDF pages [7]
According to the results in Table 6, the new 2.5 models improve greatly over previous Gemini 1.5 models and achieve state-of-the-art quality on all of those.

[P19-S00098] PDF pages [8]
Gemini's multilingual capabilities have also undergone a profound evolution since 1.5, which already encompassed over 400 languages via pretraining.

[P19-S00099] PDF pages [8]
This transformation stems from a holistic strategy, meticulously refining pre- and post-training data quality, advancing tokenization techniques, innovating core modeling, and executing targeted capability hillclimbing.

[P19-S00100] PDF pages [8]
The impact is particularly striking in Indic and Chinese, Japanese and Korean languages, where dedicated optimizations in data quality and evaluation have unlocked dramatic gains in both quality and decoding speed.

[P19-S00101] PDF pages [8]
Consequently, users benefit from significantly enhanced language adherence, responses designed to faithfully respect the requested output language, and a robust improvement in generative quality and factuality across languages, solidifying Gemini's reliability across diverse linguistic contexts.

[P19-S00102] PDF pages [8]
While Gemini 1.5 was focused on native audio understanding tasks such as transcription, translation, summarization and question-answering, in addition to understanding, Gemini 2.5 was trained to perform audio generation tasks such as text-to-speech or native audio-visual to audio out dialog.

[P19-S00103] PDF pages [8]
To enable low-latency streaming dialog, we incorporated causal audio representations that also allow streaming audio into and out of Gemini 2.5.

[P19-S00104] PDF pages [8]
These capabilities derive from an increased amount of pre-training data spanning over 200 languages, and development of improved post-training recipes.

[P19-S00105] PDF pages [8]
Finally, through our improved post-training recipes, we have integrated advanced capabilities such as thinking, affective dialog, contextual awareness and tool use into Gemini's native audio models.

[P19-S00106] PDF pages [8]
We have significantly expanded both our pretraining and post-training video understanding data, improving the audio-visual and temporal understanding capabilities of the model.

[P19-S00107] PDF pages [8]
We have also trained our models so that they perform competitively with 66 instead of 258 visual tokens per frame, enabling using about 3 hours of video instead of 1h within a 1M tokens context window3.

[P19-S00108] PDF pages [8]
Two new applications that were not previously possible, but that have been unlocked as a result of these changes are: creating an interactive app from a video (such as a quiz to test students' understanding of the video content) and creating a p5.js animation to show the key concepts from the video.

[P19-S00109] PDF pages [8]
Our recent blog post (Baddepudi et al., 2025) shows examples of these applications.

[P19-S00110] PDF pages [8]
Gemini Deep Research (Gemini Team, Google, 2024) is an agent built on top of the Gemini 2.5 Pro model designed to strategically browse the web and provide informed answers to even the most niche user queries.

[P19-S00111] PDF pages [8]
The agent is optimized to perform task prioritization, and is also able to identify when it reaches a dead-end when browsing.

[P19-S00112] PDF pages [8]
We have massively improved the capabilities of Gemini Deep Research since its initial launch in December 2024.

[P19-S00113] PDF pages [8]
As evidence of that, performance of Gemini Deep Research on the Humanity's Last Exam benchmark (Phan et al., 2025) has gone from 7.95% in December 2024 to the SoTA score of 26.9% and 32.4% with higher compute (June 2025).

[P19-S00114] PDF pages [9]
On the way to Gemini 2.5 Pro, we experimented with our training recipe, and tested a small number of these experimental models with users.

[P19-S00115] PDF pages [9]
We have already discussed Gemini 2.0 Flash Thinking (see Section 2.5).

[P19-S00116] PDF pages [9]
We will now discuss some of the other models briefly.

[P19-S00117] PDF pages [9]
In February 2025, we released an experimental version of Gemini 2.0 Pro.

[P19-S00118] PDF pages [9]
At the time, it had the strongest coding performance of any model in the Gemini model family, as well as the best understanding and world knowledge.

[P19-S00119] PDF pages [9]
It also came with our largest context window at 2 million tokens, which enabled it to comprehensively analyze and understand vast amounts of information.

[P19-S00120] PDF pages [9]
For further information about Gemini 2.0 Pro, please see our earlier blog posts (Kavukcuoglu, 2025; Mallick and Kilpatrick, 2025).

[P19-S00121] PDF pages [9]
Gemini 2.0 Flash Native Image Generation Model In March 2025, we released an experimental version of Gemini 2.0 Flash Native Image Generation.

[P19-S00122] PDF pages [9]
It has brought to the users new capabilities as a result of a strong integration between the Gemini model and image-generation capabilities, enabling new experiences related to image generation & image editing via natural-language prompting.

[P19-S00123] PDF pages [9]
Capabilities such as multi-step conversational editing or interleaved text-image generation are very natural in such a setting, and horizontal transfer related to multi-language coverage immediately allowed such experiences to happen across all the languages supported by the Gemini models.

[P19-S00124] PDF pages [9]
Native image generation turns Gemini into a multimodal creation partner and enables Gemini to express ideas through both text and images, and to seamlessly move between the two.

[P19-S00125] PDF pages [9]
With Gemini 2.5, the Controllable TTS and Native Audio Dialog capabilities are available as separate options on AI Studio (Generate Media and Stream sections respectively).

[P19-S00126] PDF pages [9]
Our Gemini 2.5 Preview TTS Pro and Flash models support more than 80 languages with the speech style controlled by a free formatted prompt which can specify style, emotion, pace, etc, while also being capable of following finer-grained steering instructions specified in the transcript.

[P19-S00127] PDF pages [9]
Notably, Gemini 2.5 Preview TTS can generate speech with multiple speakers, which enables the creation of podcasts as used in NotebookLM Audio Overviews (Wang, 2024).

[P19-S00128] PDF pages [9]
Our Gemini 2.5 Flash Preview Native Audio Dialog model uses native audio generation, which enables the same level of style, pacing and accent control as available in our controllable TTS offering.

[P19-S00129] PDF pages [9]
Our dialog model supports tool use and function calling, and is available in more than 24 languages.

[P19-S00130] PDF pages [9]
With native audio understanding and generation capabilities, it can understand and respond appropriately to the user's tone.

[P19-S00131] PDF pages [9]
This model is also capable of understanding when to respond to the user, and when not to respond, ignoring background and non-device directed audio.

[P19-S00132] PDF pages [9]
Finally, we also offer an advanced 'Thinking' variant that effectively handles more complex queries and provides more robust and reasoned responses in exchange for some additional latency.

[P19-S00133] PDF pages [9]
In June 2025, we released an experimental version of Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite (gemini-2.5-flashlite-preview-06-17).

[P19-S00134] PDF pages [9, 10]
It comes with the same capabilities that make Gemini 2.5 helpful, including the ability to turn thinking on at different budgets, connecting to tools like Google Search and code execution, support for multimodal inputs and a 1 million-token context length.

[P19-S00135] PDF pages [10]
Our goal was to provide an economical model class which provides ultra-low-latency capabilities and high throughput per dollar, echoing the initial release of 2.0 Flash-Lite (Google DeepMind, 2025b; Mallick and Kilpatrick, 2025).

[P19-S00136] PDF pages [10]
To advance Gemini's capabilities towards solving hard reasoning problems, we developed a novel reasoning approach, called Deep Think, that naturally blends in parallel thinking techniques during response generation.

[P19-S00137] PDF pages [10]
Deep Think enables Gemini to creatively produce multiple hypotheses and carefully critique them before arriving at the final answer, achieving state-of-the-art performances in challenging benchmarks such as Olympiad math (USAMO 2025), competitive coding (LiveCodeBench), and multimodality (MMMU), see more details at (Doshi, 2025b).

[P19-S00138] PDF pages [10]
We announced Gemini 2.5 Deep Think at Google I/O and launched an experimental version to trusted testers and advanced users in June 2025.

[P19-S00139] PDF pages [11]
SWE-bench verified numbers correspond to the “multiple attempts” setting reported in Table 3.

[P19-S00140] PDF pages [11]
We will now examine the performance of the Gemini 2.X model family across a wide range of benchmarks.

[P19-S00141] PDF pages [11]
We will first compare the performance of the Gemini 2.X models to the earlier Gemini 1.5 Pro and Flash models, before we compare the performance of Gemini 2.5 Pro to other available large language models.

[P19-S00142] PDF pages [11]
With web-scale pre-training of AI models, coupled with the post-training techniques that allow policy and reward models to leverage public benchmarks, avoiding leaks and biases in the data used for pre- and post-training is a persistent challenge.

[P19-S00143] PDF pages [11]
In the development of the Gemini 2.5 series, in addition to the standard n-gram based decontamination we used in Gemini 1.5, we also employed semantic-similarity and model based decontamination procedures to help mitigate evaluation set leakage.

[P19-S00144] PDF pages [11]
To move beyond the reliance on training set decontamination, we also continue reporting on internally developed non-public benchmarks, such as HiddenMath.

[P19-S00145] PDF pages [11]
Gemini 1.5 Flash gemini-1.5-flash-002 Gemini 1.5 Pro gemini-1.5-pro-002 Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite gemini-2.0-flash-lite-001 Gemini 2.0 Flash gemini-2.0-flash-001 Gemini 2.5 Flash gemini-2.5-flash Gemini 2.5 Pro gemini-2.5-pro Table 2 | Mapping of Gemini model names to AI Studio API model IDs.

[P19-S00146] PDF pages [12]

[P19-S00147] PDF pages [12]
Gemini results: All Gemini scores are pass@1, and are “single attempt” settings unless otherwise specified.

[P19-S00148] PDF pages [12]
In the “single attempt” setting, no majority voting or parallel test-time compute is permitted, while in the “multiple attempts” setting, test-time selection of the candidate answer is allowed.

[P19-S00149] PDF pages [12]
All Gemini evaluations are run with the AI Studio API for the model id that we provide in Table 2, with default sampling settings.

[P19-S00150] PDF pages [12]
To reduce variance, we average over multiple trials for smaller benchmarks.

[P19-S00151] PDF pages [12]
Aider Polyglot scores are the pass rate average of 3 trials.

[P19-S00152] PDF pages [12]
Vibe-Eval results are reported using Gemini as a judge.

[P19-S00153] PDF pages [12]
Non-Gemini results: All the results for non-Gemini models are sourced from providers' self reported numbers unless mentioned otherwise.

[P19-S00154] PDF pages [12]
All “SWE-bench Verified” numbers follow official provider reports, which means that they are computed using different scaffoldings and infrastructure, and aren't directly comparable.

[P19-S00155] PDF pages [12]
For some evaluations, we obtain results from the external leaderboards that report results on these benchmarks.

[P19-S00156] PDF pages [12]
Results for Humanity's Last Exam results are sourced from Scale's leaderboard and results for DeepSeek are obtained from the text-only variant of the leaderboard (indicated with a ⋄in Table 4).

[P19-S00157] PDF pages [12]
For Gemini 2.0 models, the reported results are on an earlier HLE dataset (indicated with a † in Table 3).

[P19-S00158] PDF pages [12]
Aider Polyglot numbers come from the Aider leaderboard and results for SimpleQA come from this repo where available.

[P19-S00159] PDF pages [12]
Results on FACTS Grounding come from Kaggle.

[P19-S00160] PDF pages [12]
In the case of LOFT and MRCR-V2, we report results on both the 128k context length variant, as well as the 1M context length variant.

[P19-S00161] PDF pages [12]
In the 128k context length variant, we measure performance on contexts up to 128k, while for the 1M context length variant, we report performance on context lengths of exactly 1M.

[P19-S00162] PDF pages [12]
More details on all benchmarks, including subsets and how scores were obtained can be found in Table 11 in Appendix 8.1.

[P19-S00163] PDF pages [12]
As can be seen in Table 3, and Figure 5, the Gemini 2.5 models excel at coding tasks such as LiveCodeBench, Aider Polyglot and SWE-bench Verified, and represent a marked improvement over previous models.

[P19-S00164] PDF pages [12]
In addition to coding performance, Gemini 2.5 models are noticeably better at math and reasoning tasks than Gemini 1.5 models: performance on AIME 2025 is 88.0% for Gemini 2.5 Pro compared to 17.5% for Gemini 1.5 Pro, while performance on GPQA (diamond) went from 58.1% for Gemini 1.5 Pro to 86.4%.

[P19-S00165] PDF pages [12]
Performance on image understanding tasks has also increased significantly.

[P19-S00166] PDF pages [12]
It is also interesting to note that the Gemini 2.5 Flash model has become the second most capable model in the Gemini family, and has overtaken not just previous Flash models, but also the Gemini 1.5 Pro model released one year ago.

[P19-S00170] PDF pages [14]

[P19-S00183] PDF pages [16]

[P19-S00184] PDF pages [16]

[P19-S00185] PDF pages [16]

[P19-S00189] PDF pages [16]
Notably, the GPP agent additionally went through the difficult (and optional) Seafoam Islands dungeon in Run 2, while in Run 1, GPP reached Cinnabar Island via Pallet Town and Route 21.

[P19-S00190] PDF pages [16]
On March 28, 2025, an independent developer not affiliated with Google, Joel Zhang, set up a Twitch stream (Gemini Plays Pokémon, or GPP) for Gemini 2.5 Pro (Gemini 2.5 Pro Exp 03-25) to play Pokémon Blue on stream (Zhang, 2025) as an experiment to better understand how well the model was capable of playing Pokémon (in a similar spirit to Claude Plays Pokémon, see Anthropic 2025).

[P19-S00191] PDF pages [16]
In this initial run through the game, the goal was to live-stream the development process of an agentic harness capable of playing the full game (and in particular the minimal transformation of vision to text necessary to do so), see Figure 14 for a description of the final agent setup.

[P19-S00192] PDF pages [16]
As such, over the course of the run, modifications were made to the setup as difficulties arose, providing a deeply interesting lens via which to analyze some of the qualitative improvements that the 2.5 Pro model has made, particularly in the regimes of solving long reasoning problems and agentic capabilities over extended time horizons.

[P19-S00193] PDF pages [16]
Around 1 month later, on May 2, 2025, Gemini 2.5 Pro completed the game after 813 hours and entered the Hall of Fame to become the Pokémon League Champion!

[P19-S00194] PDF pages [16]
On May 22, 2025, GPP began a fully autonomous 2nd run through the game with Gemini 2.5 Pro (Gemini 2.5 Pro Preview 05-06) with the finalized fixed agentic harness, and progressed through the game considerably faster, completing the game in 406.5 hours (nearly exactly half the time of the first run).

[P19-S00195] PDF pages [17]
See Figure 6 for a timeline of GPP's progress through major game milestones to game completion.

[P19-S00196] PDF pages [17]
We report # hours to each milestone in order to normalize for the amount of time models take per action.

[P19-S00197] PDF pages [17]
Gemini 2.5 Pro showcased many impressive capabilities associated with reasoning and long-term planning while playing Pokémon.

[P19-S00198] PDF pages [17]
We will now discuss two in particular, but for more examples, see Appendix 8.2.

[P19-S00199] PDF pages [17]
Long Context Agentic Tooling Within the agent scaffolding, GPP has access to two agentic tools (see Figure 14).

[P19-S00200] PDF pages [17]
These prompted versions of Gemini 2.5 Pro, hereafter pathfinder and boulder_puzzle_strategist, have been able to: 1.

[P19-S00201] PDF pages [17]
Solve complex spinner puzzles in one shot (for instance in Rocket Hideout), 2.

[P19-S00202] PDF pages [17]
Solve the step-constrained multi-map puzzle of the Safari Zone, 3.

[P19-S00203] PDF pages [17]
Find long pathways through complex mazes like Route 13, 4.

[P19-S00204] PDF pages [17]
Solve boulder puzzles across long distances in Victory Road and the Seafoam Islands.

[P19-S00205] PDF pages [17]
Each task requires reasoning over a long context - the pathfinder model would often have to reason over contexts of 100K+ tokens, and find paths up to 50 actions in length (in the extreme case, paths consisting of up to 150 actions have also been found!

[P19-S00206] PDF pages [17]
Long Horizon Task Coherence While Gemini 2.5 Pro is impressive in a more local sense, the agent also exhibited remarkable long-term task coherence in achieving global, high-level goals in the face of real and hallucinated setbacks towards making forward progress.

[P19-S00207] PDF pages [17]
Because the agent is able to change goals at will, and will generally follow those goals as long as needed, it is extremely impressive that the agent can satisfy numerous requirements for tactical, necessary goals, such as acquiring Hidden Moves, as well as maintain enough strategic task coherence to beat the entire game and become the Pokémon Champion.

[P19-S00208] PDF pages [17]
Where does 2.5 Pro struggle while playing Pokémon?

[P19-S00209] PDF pages [17]
In addition to more standard hallucination issues (which interestingly were plausibly reduced in Run 2 by explicitly prompting the model to act as a player completely new to the game, see Appendix 8.2 for more details), there are a few particular points of struggle we would like to emphasize.

[P19-S00210] PDF pages [17]
Screen reading While obtaining excellent benchmark numbers on real-world vision tasks, 2.5 Pro struggled to utilize the raw pixels of the Game Boy screen directly, though it could occasionally take cues from information on the pixels.

[P19-S00211] PDF pages [17]
As a result, it was necessary for the required information from the screen to be translated into a text format in the agent framework, using information from the game's RAM state.

[P19-S00212] PDF pages [17]
During one portion of the game, the developer tested an ablation where all vision was completely removed from the model context - the model was able to function roughly as well as without the vision information, suggesting that most of the performance does not significantly depend on the visual input.

[P19-S00213] PDF pages [17]
Long Context Reasoning Gemini 2.5 Pro's state-of-the-art long context performance for both reasoning and retrieval tasks (see Tables 3 and 4) was a cornerstone of the GPP agent's success.

[P19-S00214] PDF pages [17, 18]
Its ability to reason over a 100k token context was instrumental for leveraging the complex toolset and maintaining a relatively coherent strategy (e.g., optimal balance of performance, planning quality, and information recall.)

[P19-S00215] PDF pages [18]
While Gemini 2.5 Pro supports 1M+ token context, making effective use of it for agents presents a new research frontier.

[P19-S00216] PDF pages [18]
In this agentic setup, it was observed that as the context grew significantly beyond 100k tokens, the agent showed a tendency toward favoring repeating actions from its vast history rather than synthesizing novel plans.

[P19-S00217] PDF pages [18]
This phenomenon, albeit anecdotal, highlights an important distinction between long-context for retrieval and long-context for multi-step, generative reasoning.

[P19-S00218] PDF pages [18]
Teaching an agent to effectively plan and avoid such loops over massive past trajectories of context is an exciting and active area of research; the co-design of agent scaffolds and models to unlock the full potential of million-token context is an intriguing research direction and one of our primary focuses.

[P19-S00219] PDF pages [18]
Gemini 2.5 Pro excels at transforming diverse, often unstructured, inputs into interactive and functional applications.

[P19-S00220] PDF pages [18]
For instance, it can take a PDF script of a play and generate a tool that allows drama students to practice their lines.

[P19-S00221] PDF pages [18]
Gemini 2.5 Pro can also take an uploaded photograph of a bookshelf and create a curated book recommendation application.

[P19-S00222] PDF pages [18]
Gemini 2.5 Pro can utilize its underlying spatial understanding capability and convert images into a structural representation like HTML or SVG.

[P19-S00223] PDF pages [18]
In Figure 16 in Appendix 8.4, we show a comparison of Gemini 1.5 Pro and Gemini 2.5 Pro on an image-to-svg task, where Gemini 2.5 Pro reconstructs much more visual details and the spatial arrangements of objects better resembles the original image.

[P19-S00224] PDF pages [18]
Furthermore, Gemini 2.5 Pro demonstrates strong skills in generating sophisticated simulations and visualizations, ranging from interactive solar system models (source) to the creative rendering of abstract mathematical concepts, such as drawing a logo using Fourier series (source).

[P19-S00225] PDF pages [18]
This capability extends to the development of tools that intersect creativity and utility: we see examples of specialized applications like a custom cartography tool or use cases that generate photorealistic 3D user interfaces from descriptive text and reference images, complete with appropriate styling and interactivity (source).

[P19-S00226] PDF pages [18]
Collectively, these examples illustrate that Gemini 2.5 Pro is not just a useful coding and writing assistant, but excels at a wide range of complex tasks, ranging from those relevant for education to creative expression.

[P19-S00227] PDF pages [18]
The model empowers users to rapidly prototype specialized utilities, develop engaging educational content, and realize intricate creative visions with a high degree of sophistication.

[P19-S00228] PDF pages [18]
As a final example of what Gemini can do, we note that Gemini (or a custom version of Gemini) is now incorporated into a wide variety of Google products.

[P19-S00229] PDF pages [18]
These include, but are not limited to, AI Overviews and AI Mode within Google Search, Project Astra, the audiovisual-to-audio dialog agent, Gemini Deep Research, the research assistant discussed in Section 2.7, NotebookLM, the tool capable of generating podcasts and audio overviews from even the most obscure inputs, Project Mariner, the web browsing agent, and Google's coding agent, Jules.

[P19-S00230] PDF pages [19]
We're committed to developing Gemini responsibly, innovating on safety and security alongside capa- bilities.

[P19-S00231] PDF pages [19]
We describe our current approach in this section, which includes how we train and evaluate our models, focusing on automated red teaming, going through held-out assurance evaluations on present-day risks, and evaluating the potential for dangerous capabilities in order to proactively anticipate new and long-term risks.

[P19-S00232] PDF pages [19]
Our Process (Section 5.1): Begin here to understand our overall safety methodology.

[P19-S00233] PDF pages [19]
Policies and Desiderata (Section 5.2): Next, dive into the safety criteria we use to evaluate and optimize our systems.

[P19-S00234] PDF pages [19]
Training for Safety (Section 5.3): Discover how we incorporate safety into pre-training and post-training.

[P19-S00235] PDF pages [19]
Results from Development Evaluations (Section 5.4): Results on our development evaluations for policies and desiderata.

[P19-S00236] PDF pages [19]
Automated Red Teaming (Section 5.5): A description and results from our automated red teaming work for safety and security.

[P19-S00237] PDF pages [19]
Memorization & Privacy (Section 5.6): Our analysis of memorization and privacy risks.

[P19-S00238] PDF pages [19]
Assurance Evaluations and Frontier Safety Framework (Section 5.7): We dive into our held-out evaluations and tests for dangerous capabilities.

[P19-S00239] PDF pages [19]
External Safety Testing (Section 5.8): Learn what independent testers discovered about our system's safety.

[P19-S00240] PDF pages [19]
We aim for Gemini to adhere to specific safety, security, and responsibility criteria.

[P19-S00241] PDF pages [19]
These cover what Gemini should not do (e.g., encourage violence), and what Gemini should do (e.g., respond in a helpful way when possible instead of refusing, provide multiple perspectives when consensus does not exist).

[P19-S00242] PDF pages [19]
We also leverage automated red teaming to identify cases where the model fails to respond in a safe or helpful manner.

[P19-S00243] PDF pages [19]
These failure cases are used to improve evaluations and training data.

[P19-S00244] PDF pages [19]
Once the model is trained, we run assurance evaluations that we then use for review and release decisions.

[P19-S00245] PDF pages [19]
Importantly, these are conducted by a group outside of the model development team, and datasets are held out.

[P19-S00246] PDF pages [19]
Furthermore, for models where there are new capabilities or a significant performance improvement, we engage independent external groups, including domain experts and a government body, to further test the model to identify blind spots.

[P19-S00247] PDF pages [19]
Finally, The Google DeepMind Responsibility and Safety Council (RSC), our governance body, reviews initial ethics and safety assessments on novel model capabilities in order to provide feedback and guidance during model development.

[P19-S00248] PDF pages [19]
The RSC also reviews metrics on the models' performance via assurance evals and informs release decisions.

[P19-S00249] PDF pages [20]
The Gemini safety policies align with Google's standard framework which prevents our our Generative AI models from generating specific types of harmful content, including: 1.

[P19-S00250] PDF pages [20]
Hate speech (e.g., dehumanizing members of protected groups) 3.

[P19-S00251] PDF pages [20]
Dangerous content (e.g., promoting suicide, or instructing in activities that could cause real-world harm) 4.

[P19-S00252] PDF pages [20]
Medical advice that runs contrary to scientific or medical consensus These policies apply across modalities.

[P19-S00253] PDF pages [20]
For example, they are meant to minimize the extent to which Gemini generates outputs such as suicide instructions or revealing harmful personal data, irrespective of input modality.

[P19-S00254] PDF pages [20]
From a security standpoint, beyond limiting revealing private information, Gemini strives to protect users from cyberattacks, for example, by being robust to prompt injection attacks.

[P19-S00255] PDF pages [20]
Defining what not to do is only part of the safety story - it is equally important to define what we do want the model to do: 1.

[P19-S00256] PDF pages [20]
Help the user: fulfill the user request; only refuse if it is not possible to find a response that fulfills the user goals without violating policy.

[P19-S00257] PDF pages [20]
Assume good intent: if a refusal is necessary, articulate it respectfully without making assumptions about user intent.

[P19-S00258] PDF pages [20]
We build safety into the models though pre-and post-training approaches.

[P19-S00259] PDF pages [20]
We start by constructing metrics based on the policies and desiderata above, which we typically turn into automated evaluations that guide model development through successive model iterations.

[P19-S00260] PDF pages [20]
We use data filtering and conditional pre-training, as well as Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), and Reinforcement Learning from Human and Critic Feedback (RL*F).

[P19-S00261] PDF pages [20]
Below, we explain these approaches, and then share results across the policies and desiderata for Gemini 2.0 and Gemini 2.5 models.

[P19-S00262] PDF pages [20]
Throughout this process we strive for coverage of the safety policies described above across common model use cases.

[P19-S00263] PDF pages [20, 21]
When we find that model behavior needs improvement, either because of safety policy violations, or because the model refuses when a helpful, non-policy-violating answer exists, we use a combination of custom data generation recipes loosely inspired by Constitutional AI (Bai et al., 2022), as well as human intervention to revise responses.

[P19-S00264] PDF pages [21]
The process described here is typically refined through successive model iterations.

[P19-S00265] PDF pages [21]
We use automated evaluations on both safety and non-safety metrics to monitor impact and potential unintended regressions.

[P19-S00266] PDF pages [21]
We divide our interventions into Reward Model and Critic improvements (RM), and reinforcement learning (RL) improvements.

[P19-S00267] PDF pages [21]
For both RM and RL, similarly to SFT, we source prompts either through human-model or model-model interactions, striving for coverage of safety policies and use cases.

[P19-S00268] PDF pages [21]
For both DRM training, given a prompt set, we use custom data generation recipes to surface a representative sample of model responses.

[P19-S00269] PDF pages [21]
Humans then provide feedback on the responses, often comparing multiple potential response candidates for each query.

[P19-S00270] PDF pages [21]
This preference data is amortized in our Data Reward Model.

[P19-S00271] PDF pages [21]
Critics, on the other hand, do not require additional data, and iteration on the grading rubric can be done offline.

[P19-S00272] PDF pages [21]
Similarly to SFT, RL*F steers the model away from undesirable behavior, both in terms of content policy violations, and trains the model to be helpful.

[P19-S00273] PDF pages [21]
RL*F is accompanied by a number of evaluations that run continuously during training to monitor for safety and other metrics.

[P19-S00274] PDF pages [21]
Our primary safety evaluations assess the extent to which our models follow our content safety policies.

[P19-S00275] PDF pages [21]
We also track how helpful the model is in fulfilling requests that should be fulfilled, and how objective or respectful its tone is.

[P19-S00276] PDF pages [21]
Compared to Gemini 1.5 models, the 2.0 models are substantially safer.

[P19-S00277] PDF pages [21]
However, they overrefused on a wide variety of benign user requests.

[P19-S00278] PDF pages [21]
In Gemini 2.5, we have focused on improving helpfulness / instruction following (IF), specifically to reduce refusals on such benign requests.

[P19-S00279] PDF pages [21]
This means that we train Gemini to answer questions as accurately as possible, while prioritizing safety and minimising unhelpful responses.

[P19-S00280] PDF pages [21]
New models are more willing to engage with prompts where previous models may have over-refused, and this nuance can impact our automated safety scores.

[P19-S00281] PDF pages [21]
We expect variation in our automated safety evaluations results, which is why we review flagged content to check for egregious or dangerous material.

[P19-S00282] PDF pages [21]
Our manual review confirmed losses were overwhelmingly either a) false positives or b) not egregious.

[P19-S00283] PDF pages [21]
Furthermore, this review confirmed losses are narrowly concentrated around explicit requests to produce sexually suggestive content or hateful content, mostly in the context of creative use-cases (e.g. historical fiction).

[P19-S00284] PDF pages [21]
We have not observed increased violations outside these specific contexts.

[P19-S00285] PDF pages [21]
To complement human red teaming and our static evaluations, we make extensive use of automated red teaming (ART) to dynamically evaluate Gemini at scale (Beutel et al., 2024; Perez et al., 2022; Samvelyan et al., 2024).

[P19-S00286] PDF pages [21]
This allows us to significantly increase our coverage and understanding of potential risks, as well as rapidly develop model improvements to make Gemini safer and more helpful.

[P19-S00287] PDF pages [22]
Metric Gemini 1.5 Flash 002 Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite vs.

[P19-S00288] PDF pages [22]
A down arrow (↓) indicates a reduction in the number of policy violations (better), while an up arrow (↑) indicates an improvement for Tone and Helpfulness / Instruction Following.

[P19-S00289] PDF pages [22]
We formulate ART as a multi-agent game between populations of attackers and the target Gemini model being evaluated.

[P19-S00290] PDF pages [22]
The goal of the attackers is to elicit responses from the target model which satisfy some defined objectives (e.g. if the response violates a safety policy, or is unhelpful).

[P19-S00291] PDF pages [22]
These interactions are scored by various judges (e.g. using a set of policies), with the resulting scores used by the attackers as a reward signal to optimize their attacks.

[P19-S00292] PDF pages [22]
Our attackers evaluate Gemini in a black-box setting, using natural language queries without access to the model's internal parameters.

[P19-S00293] PDF pages [22]
This focus on naturalistic interactions ensures our automated red teaming is more reflective of real-world use cases and challenges.

[P19-S00294] PDF pages [22]
Attackers are prompted Gemini models, while our judges are a mixture of prompted and finetuned Gemini models.

[P19-S00295] PDF pages [22]
To direct the attackers and judges, we use various seeds including policy guidelines, trending topics, and past escalations.

[P19-S00296] PDF pages [22]
Policies are sourced from: (1) policy experts who collaborate with us to incorporate their policies into the judges, and (2) Gemini itself which generates synthetic guidelines that are reviewed by humans and then used.

[P19-S00297] PDF pages [22]
We also work with internal teams to evaluate the most relevant trending topics in the world and corresponding potential risks.

[P19-S00298] PDF pages [22]
These dual approaches allow us to complement human expertise with automation, enabling red teaming to evaluate known and unknown issues at scale.

[P19-S00299] PDF pages [22]
The generality of our approach has allowed us to rapidly scale red teaming to a growing number of areas including not just policy violations (Section 5.4), but also areas such as tone, helpfulness, and neutrality.

[P19-S00300] PDF pages [22]
For each area, we are able to generate thousands of informative examples per hour (e.g. prompts which elicit unsafe or biased responses from Gemini).

[P19-S00301] PDF pages [22]
This has resulted in the discovery of novel issues prior to model and product releases, and helped inform policy development/refinement.

[P19-S00302] PDF pages [22]
Furthermore, automated red teaming has significantly accelerated the turnaround time from discovering to mitigating issues thanks to the rapid creation of evaluation and training sets, as well as informing product-level mitigations prior to releases.

[P19-S00303] PDF pages [22]
As a concrete example of the use and impact of automated red teaming, we highlight the consistent reduction in helpfulness violations discovered by ART, with Gemini 2.5 Flash and 2.5 Pro being our most helpful models to-date while maintaining robust safety metrics.

[P19-S00304] PDF pages [23]
Our evaluation measures Gemini's susceptibility to indirect prompt injection attacks.

[P19-S00305] PDF pages [23]
As illustrated in Figure 7, we specifically focus on a scenario in which a third party hides malicious instructions in external retrieved data, in order to manipulate Gemini into taking unauthorized actions through function calling.

[P19-S00306] PDF pages [23]
In our scenario, the specific function calls available to Gemini allow it to summarize a user's latest emails, and to send emails on their behalf.

[P19-S00307] PDF pages [23]
The attacker's specific objective is to manipulate the model to invoke a send email function call that discreetly exfiltrates sensitive information from conversation history.

[P19-S00308] PDF pages [23]
The attacker sends the user an email whose contents prompt Gemini to send user secrets to an attacker-controlled email address.

[P19-S00309] PDF pages [23]
When the user requests a summary of this email, it is retrieved into context.

[P19-S00310] PDF pages [23]
The attack is successful if Gemini executes the malicious prompt contained in the email, resulting in the unauthorized disclosure of sensitive information to the adversary.

[P19-S00311] PDF pages [23]
The attack is unsuccessful if Gemini complies with its intended functionality of only following user instructions and provides a simple summary of the email.

[P19-S00312] PDF pages [23]
For evaluation, we use Gemini to generate synthetic conversations between a user and an AI assistant containing references to simulated private user information.

[P19-S00313] PDF pages [23]
These synthetic conversations emulate how a user might discuss private information with the agent.

[P19-S00314] PDF pages [23]
Manually generating prompt injections is an inefficient process as it relies on humans writing triggers, submitting them to Gemini, and using the responses to refine the prompts.

[P19-S00315] PDF pages [23]
Instead, we develop several attacks that automate the process of generating malicious prompts: • Actor Critic: This attack uses an attacker-controlled model to generate suggestions for triggers.

[P19-S00316] PDF pages [23]
These are passed to the model under attack, which returns a probability score of a successful attack.

[P19-S00317] PDF pages [23]
Based on this probability, the attack model refines the trigger.

[P19-S00318] PDF pages [23]
This process repeats until the attack model converges to a successful and generalized trigger.

[P19-S00319] PDF pages [24]
If the model recognises the request as suspicious and does not comply, the attack adds random tokens to the end of the trigger and measures the new probability of the attack succeeding.

[P19-S00320] PDF pages [24]
If the probability increases, these random tokens are kept, otherwise they are removed, and the process repeats until the combination of the trigger and random appended tokens results in a successful attack.

[P19-S00321] PDF pages [24]
We adapt this attack, making several adjustments to target security violations.

[P19-S00322] PDF pages [24]
Like Actor Critic, this attack searches in the natural language space; however we assume the attacker cannot access probability scores from the model under attack, only the text samples that are generated.

[P19-S00323] PDF pages [24]
After constructing prompt injections using these methods, we evaluate them on a held-out set of synthetic conversation histories containing simulated private user information, which for the results reported below are synthetic passport numbers.

[P19-S00324] PDF pages [24]
We report the best attack success rate (ASR) achieved across these prompt injections.

[P19-S00325] PDF pages [24]
ASR represents the percentage of simulated private information that is successfully exfiltrated to the attacker - because the attacker has no prior knowledge of the conversation history, the prompt injection must generalize across conversation histories to achieve a high ASR, making this a harder task than eliciting generic unaligned responses from the model.

[P19-S00326] PDF pages [24]
The table below summarizes the results.

[P19-S00327] PDF pages [24]
For both Gemini 2.0 Flash and Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite, we find that they are more resilient against our Actor Critic and Beam Search attacks.

[P19-S00328] PDF pages [24]
In Actor Critic, which uses iteratively more persuasive natural language prompt injections, ASRs reduced substantially compared with both Gemini 1.5 Flash; while in Beam Search which primarily relies on discovering random tokens resulting in successful attacks, the ASR also reduced noticeably.

[P19-S00329] PDF pages [24]
However, for TAP, which leverages more creative natural language scenarios like role-playing to attack the model, the ASR on Gemini 2.0 Flash increased by 16.2% on already very high ASRs for Gemini 1.5 Flash.

[P19-S00330] PDF pages [24]
Our results indicate that Gemini 2.0 models are becoming more resilient to some classes of prompt injection attacks in environments containing private user data.

[P19-S00331] PDF pages [24]
However, improved model capabilities of Gemini 2.0 versus Gemini 1.5 also enable attackers to leverage the model's ability to create natural language attacks like TAP.

[P19-S00332] PDF pages [24]
The lower ASRs on Actor Critic and TAP against Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite is likely the result of comparatively lower capability of the smaller Flash-Lite model compared to Gemini 2.0 Flash, rather than an indication of greater internal resilience.

[P19-S00333] PDF pages [24]
In Gemini 2.5 Flash and Gemini 2.5 Pro, we have observed greater resilience against all three of our attack techniques across the board, despite significantly increased model capabilities.

[P19-S00334] PDF pages [24]
This is a result of the security adversarial training against indirect prompt injection attacks we added in Gemini 2.5, further details for which can be found in the white paper (Shi et al., 2025) we recently released.

[P19-S00335] PDF pages [24]
However the Gemini 2.5 Pro model is still less resilient compared to Gemini 2.5 Flash, showing that increased model capabilities in Pro still constrain our mitigations.

[P19-S00336] PDF pages [24]
We are continuing to evolve our adversarial evaluations to accurately measure and monitor the resilience of increasingly capable Gemini models, as well as our adversarial training techniques to further improve the security of our models.

[P19-S00337] PDF pages [24]
Large language models are known to potentially produce near-copies of some training examples (Biderman et al., 2023; Carlini et al., 2022; Ippolito et al., 2022; Nasr et al., 2023).

[P19-S00338] PDF pages [25]
Attack Technique Gemini 1.5 Flash 002 Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite vs.

[P19-S00339] PDF pages [25]
ASRs are reported as a percentage of 500 held-out scenarios where the best-performing prompt injection trigger successfully exfiltrated sensitive information; lower ASRs are better.

[P19-S00340] PDF pages [25]
This memorization rate is defined to be the ratio of model generations that match the training data of all model generations, approximated using a sufficiently large sample size.

[P19-S00341] PDF pages [25]
In this report, we follow the methodology described in Gemini Team (2024).

[P19-S00342] PDF pages [25]
Specifically, we sample over 700,000 documents from the training data, distributed across different corpora, and use this sample to test for discoverable extraction (Nasr et al., 2023) using a prefix of length 50 and a suffix of length 50.

[P19-S00343] PDF pages [25]
We characterize text as either exactly memorized if all tokens in the continuation match the source suffix or approximately memorized if they match up to an edit distance of 10%.

[P19-S00344] PDF pages [25]
We order these models in reverse chronological order, with the newest model on the left.

[P19-S00345] PDF pages [25]
We find that the Gemini 2.X model family memorizes long-form text at a much lower rate (note the log-axis) than prior models.

[P19-S00346] PDF pages [25]
Moreover, we find that a larger proportion of text is characterized as approximately memorized by the Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite and Gemini 2.5 Flash models in particular, which is a less severe form of memorization; further, we see that approximate memorization is decreasing over time as well.

[P19-S00347] PDF pages [25]
This continues a trend of a relative increase in approximate memorization to exact memorization (c.f.

[P19-S00348] PDF pages [25]
Next, we study the rate at which the content that was characterized as memorized using our definitions also are characterized as containing potentially personal information.

[P19-S00349] PDF pages [25]
To characterize this, we use the Google Cloud Sensitive Data Protection (SDP) service.4 This tool uses broad detection rules to classify text into many types of potentially personal and sensitive information.

[P19-S00350] PDF pages [25]
SDP is designed to have high recall and does not consider the context in which the information may appear, which leads to many false positives.

[P19-S00351] PDF pages [25]
Thus, we are likely overestimating the true amount of potentially personal information contained in the outputs classified as memorized.

[P19-S00352] PDF pages [25]
SDP also provides broad severity levels: low, medium, and high.

[P19-S00353] PDF pages [25]
We classify text as personal if SDP classifies it as personal information at any severity level.

[P19-S00354] PDF pages [25]
We observed no personal information in the outputs characterized as memorization for Gemini 2.X model family models; this indicates a low rate of personal data in outputs classified as memorization that are below our detection thresholds.

[P19-S00355] PDF pages [25]
Here, we can also clearly see the trend of reduced memorization rates overall.

[P19-S00356] PDF pages [26]
Gemini 2.X model family memorize significantly less than all prior models.

[P19-S00357] PDF pages [26]
We observed no instances of personal information being included in outputs classified as memorization for Gemini 2.X, and no instances of high-severity personal data in outputs classified as memorization in prior Gemini models.

[P19-S00358] PDF pages [26]
In particular, they designed a “divergence attack” that sometimes breaks the alignment of a language model by filling its context with many repeated tokens.

[P19-S00359] PDF pages [26]
We evaluate Gemini 2.X model family models to understand their susceptibility to diverging, and in particular, to emitting data classified as memorization as a result of this attack.

[P19-S00360] PDF pages [26]
We follow the same test as in Gemini Team (2024).

[P19-S00361] PDF pages [26]
We prompt the model a total of 3750 times, evenly split across 125 different single-token characters.

[P19-S00362] PDF pages [26]
We first classify when the model returns diverged outputs, and in these cases, we then determine how many of these outputs match training data, i.e., are classified as memorization.

[P19-S00363] PDF pages [26]
Overall, we find that divergence occurs roughly 69% of the time for Gemini 2.0 Flash + Flash-Lite and roughly 59% of the time for the Gemini 2.5 model family.

[P19-S00364] PDF pages [26]
In cases where the model did not diverge, we often observed it was because the model refused to repeat content or because the model was confused by the request.

[P19-S00365] PDF pages [26]
When divergence was successful, we found that the rate of text emitted classified as memorization was roughly 0.2%.

[P19-S00366] PDF pages [26]
In these cases, we found that the text was often boilerplate code or web content.

[P19-S00367] PDF pages [26]
Assurance evaluations are our 'arms-length' internal evaluations for responsibility governance decision making (Weidinger et al., 2024).

[P19-S00368] PDF pages [26]
They are conducted separately from the model development team, to inform decision-making about release.

[P19-S00369] PDF pages [26]
High-level findings are fed back to the model development team, but individual prompt sets are held-out to prevent overfitting.

[P19-S00370] PDF pages [26]
Our baseline assurance evaluations are conducted for model release decision-making.

[P19-S00371] PDF pages [26]
They look at model behaviour related to content policies, unfair bias and any modality-specific risk areas.

[P19-S00372] PDF pages [26, 27]
They were performed for 2.5 Pro and 2.5 Flash in line with the previous Gemini 2.0 releases and the Gemini 1.5 tech report, covering all modalities in the Gemini 2.5 model family.

[P19-S00373] PDF pages [27]
Dataset composition is an essential component of our assurance evaluation robustness.

[P19-S00374] PDF pages [27]
As the risk landscape changes and modalities mature, we update our adversarial datasets to maintain quality and representativeness.

[P19-S00375] PDF pages [27]
This constant evolution of datasets can make strict comparisons between model family evaluations difficult.

[P19-S00376] PDF pages [27]
However, we provide a qualitative assessment of evaluation trends over time below.

[P19-S00377] PDF pages [27]
For child safety evaluations, we continue to see the Gemini 2.5 family of models meeting or improving upon launch thresholds, which were developed by expert teams to protect children online and meet Google's commitments to child safety across our models and Google products.

[P19-S00378] PDF pages [27]
For content policies, we see the Gemini 2.5 family of models displaying lower violation rates in most modalities than Gemini 1.5 and 2.0 families, which in turn was a significant improvement on Gemini 1.0.

[P19-S00379] PDF pages [27]
When looking at violation rates across input modalities for 2.5 Pro and 2.5 Flash (i.e. text, image, video, audio), we observe the image to text modality has a relatively higher violation rate, though the overall violation rates remained low.

[P19-S00380] PDF pages [27]
We also observed that violation rates for 2.5 Pro and 2.5 Flash tended to be slightly higher with thinking traces visible.

[P19-S00381] PDF pages [27]
Within our evaluations for unfair bias, we observed a reduction in ungrounded inferences about people in image understanding relative to Gemini 1.5.

[P19-S00382] PDF pages [27]
Ungrounded inferences are inferences that cannot be made based on the provided image and text prompt, where ideally the model would refuse to infer an answer.

[P19-S00383] PDF pages [27]
A high rate of ungrounded inferences about people may create greater risk of stereotyping, harmful associations or inaccuracies.

[P19-S00384] PDF pages [27]
Though we saw a reduction in ungrounded inferences across the board in Gemini 2.0 and 2.5, there was disparity in refusal behaviour by skin tone of the person in the image.

[P19-S00385] PDF pages [27]
We observed models tended to be more likely to make ungrounded inferences about images of people with lighter skin tones than darker skin tones.

[P19-S00386] PDF pages [27]
The Gemini 2.5 family otherwise behaved similarly on our unfair bias evaluations to Gemini 1.5.

[P19-S00387] PDF pages [27]
We continue to explore and expand our understanding of unfair bias in Gemini models.

[P19-S00388] PDF pages [27]
Findings from these evaluations were made available to teams deploying models, informing implementation of further product-level protections such as safety filtering.

[P19-S00389] PDF pages [27]
Assurance evaluation results were also reported to our Responsibility & Safety Council as part of model release review.

[P19-S00390] PDF pages [27]
Google DeepMind released its Frontier Safety Framework (FSF)(Google DeepMind, 2025a) in May 2024 and updated it in February 2025.

[P19-S00391] PDF pages [27]
The FSF comprises a number of processes and evaluations that address risks of severe harm stemming from powerful capabilities of our frontier models.

[P19-S00392] PDF pages [27]
It covers four risk domains: CBRN (chemical, biological, radiological and nuclear information risks), cybersecurity, machine learning R&D, and deceptive alignment.

[P19-S00393] PDF pages [27]
The Frontier Safety Framework involves the regular evaluation of Google's frontier models to determine whether they require heightened mitigations.

[P19-S00394] PDF pages [27]
More specifically, the FSF defines critical capability levels (CCLs) for each area, which represent capability levels where a model may pose a significant risk of severe harm without appropriate mitigations.

[P19-S00395] PDF pages [27]
When conducting FSF evaluations, we compare test results against internal alert thresholds (“early warnings”) which are set significantly below the actual CCLs.

[P19-S00396] PDF pages [27]
This built-in safety buffer helps us be proactive by signaling potential risks well before models reach CCLs.

[P19-S00397] PDF pages [27]
Concretely, our alert thresholds are designed such that if a frontier model does not reach the alert threshold for a CCL, models are unlikely to reach that CCL before the next regular testing-which we conduct at a regular cadence and also when we anticipate or see exceptional capability progress.

[P19-S00398] PDF pages [27, 28]
Our recent paper (Shah et al., Key Results for Gemini 2.5 Pro (up to version 06-05) CCL CCL reached?

[P19-S00400] PDF pages [28]
However, no current Gemini model consistently or completely enables progress through key bottleneck stages.

Across all areas covered by the Frontier Safety Framework, Critical Capability Levels (CCLs) have not been reached.

[P19-S00403] PDF pages [28]
Gemini 2.5 Pro is therefore unlikely to pose significant risk of severe harm.

For descriptions of each CCL, see main text.

While there are increased scores in some areas, we find that Gemini 2.5 Pro (up to version 06-17) does not reach any of the FSF CCLs.

[P19-S00407] PDF pages [28]

[P19-S00408] PDF pages [28]
Consistent with the FSF, we are putting in place a response plan which includes testing models' cyber capabilities more frequently and accelerating mitigations for them.

[P19-S00409] PDF pages [28]

[P19-S00411] PDF pages [29]
The model demonstrated accurate and detailed technical capabilities, potentially lowering barriers across multiple operational stages of certain harm journeys for low-resourced actors.

[P19-S00412] PDF pages [29]
While its consolidation and supplementation of fragmented procedures provides incremental uplift over what is readily available through open source search alone, it does not yet consistently or completely enable progress through key bottleneck stages, and therefore does not cross the CCL.

[P19-S00413] PDF pages [29]
Further, while Gemini 2.5 generates accurate and more detailed responses than 2.0, many of the concepts/outputs observed were already accessible through multiturn or even singleturn prompting in 2.0.

[P19-S00414] PDF pages [29]
Overview: We perform CBRN evaluations internally and via third party external testers (see section 5.8).

[P19-S00415] PDF pages [29]
Here, we report solely on internal evaluations, for which we use two different types of approaches to evaluate the models' dual-use CBRN capabilities: • Close-ended multiple choice questions (MCQs) providing a quantitative grade.

[P19-S00416] PDF pages [29]
Currently we do not run specific open-ended qualitative assessments of chemical information risks for our internal evaluations.

[P19-S00417] PDF pages [29]
However, our third party external testers include chemistry in their assessments.

[P19-S00418] PDF pages [29]
Multiple Choice Questions: The underlying assumption when using knowledge-based and reasoning MCQs is that if the model cannot answer these questions properly, it is less likely to be able to cause severe harm: the type of information in the MCQs is the type of information that is necessary, but not sufficient to help malicious actors cause severe harm.

[P19-S00419] PDF pages [29]
Examples of model performance on three external benchmarks are shown in Figure 9: i) SecureBio VMQA single-choice; ii) FutureHouse LAB-Bench presented as three subsets (ProtocolQA, Cloning Scenarios, SeqQA) (Laurent et al., 2024); and iii) Weapons of Mass Destruction Proxy (WMDP) presented as the biology and chemistry data sets (Li et al., 2024).

[P19-S00420] PDF pages [29]
Results: We observe a general trend of increasing scores, with Gemini 2.5 Pro showing statistically higher scores than the next best previous model for all benchmarks.

[P19-S00421] PDF pages [29]
Open-Ended Questions: This qualitative assessment was performed for biological, radiological and nuclear domains; it includes knowledge-based, adversarial and dual-use content.

[P19-S00422] PDF pages [29]
Questions span a range of difficulty levels, from questions a non-expert in these domains might ask, to questions that mostly an expert with a PhD plus many years of experience could pose or answer correctly.

[P19-S00423] PDF pages [29]
The prompts and scenarios span different threat journeys (e.g. types of actors, equipment used, harm intended).

[P19-S00424] PDF pages [29]
This qualitative assessment, led by domain experts, allows for better visibility of the granular improvement in science capabilities (e.g. accuracy, completeness, actionability of responses).

[P19-S00425] PDF pages [29]
Results: We observe that the same prompts used on previous models result in Gemini 2.5 Pro often generating detailed and accurate responses.

[P19-S00426] PDF pages [29]
In particular domains, some answers were technically precise and potentially actionable, but the model did not consistently or completely enable progress through all key bottleneck steps.

[P19-S00427] PDF pages [30]
In order to control for inherent model stochasticity, and position bias in selection of correct answers, we shuffled the answer choices over 100 runs for each benchmark and we report here the mean solve rate.

[P19-S00428] PDF pages [30]
While Gemini 2.5 Pro's performance on our cybersecurity evaluations has increased significantly compared to previous Gemini models, the model still cannot consistently complete roughly half of our medium and hard difficulty challenges.

[P19-S00429] PDF pages [30]
While Gemini 2.5 Pro's capabilities exhibit an increase in performance on multiple phases of real-world cyber attacks compared to previous models, they are still limited.

[P19-S00430] PDF pages [30]
The model still struggles with many of our hard challenges, which are most representative of real-world scenarios.

[P19-S00431] PDF pages [30]
The model's performance is strong enough that it has passed our early warning alert threshold.

[P19-S00432] PDF pages [30]
That is, we find it possible that subsequent revisions in the next few months could lead to a model that reaches the CCL.

[P19-S00433] PDF pages [30]

[P19-S00434] PDF pages [30]
Overview: To estimate capabilities relevant to these CCLs, we use our existing suite of cybersecurity challenges (Phuong et al., 2024) combined with a new benchmark described below.

[P19-S00435] PDF pages [30]
We consider difficulty levels ranging from easy (at the level of a college student), medium (at the level of a graduate student), and hard (at the level of an experienced cybersecurity professional).

[P19-S00436] PDF pages [30, 31]
Existing challenges: These take the form of capture-the-flag evaluations covering three difficulty levels: easy (InterCode-CTF, Yang et al. (2023), medium (our in-house suite), and hard (Hack the Box).

[P19-S00437] PDF pages [31]
See Phuong et al. (2024) for details.

[P19-S00438] PDF pages [31]
These evaluations are only relevant to Autonomy Level 1 (poor performance on these evaluations rules out capabilities sufficient for full automation), but not Uplift Level 1, since humans could still be accelerated on attack phases not covered.

[P19-S00439] PDF pages [31]
Key skills benchmark: Complementing our existing suite, we created a new evaluation framework (Rodriguez et al., 2025) that increases the realism as well as coverage across and within the attack chain of our cyber evaluations.

[P19-S00440] PDF pages [31]
We drew from real-world threat intelligence and input from experts to identify a representative set of cyber attacks.

[P19-S00441] PDF pages [31]
From there, we determined the key skills areas that would be required to execute them.

[P19-S00442] PDF pages [31]
Reconnaissance (the application of finding and researching different knowledge and data and applying it in a cybersecurity context); 2.

[P19-S00443] PDF pages [31]
Tool development (the ability to design and create software that is cybersecurity-specific); 3.

[P19-S00444] PDF pages [31]
Tool usage (the ability to leverage common and cybersecurity-specific tools to achieve routine instrumental cyber goals); 4.

[P19-S00445] PDF pages [31]
Operational security (the skill of remaining hidden during and after a cyber operation).

[P19-S00446] PDF pages [31]
We instantiate this benchmark by mapping 48 challenges from an external vendor to this specification.

[P19-S00447] PDF pages [31]
We also use these evaluations as a proxy for uplift capability, for Cyber Uplift Level 1.

[P19-S00448] PDF pages [31]
Even partial automation of these key skills could mean fewer resources are needed for sophisticated cyberattacks.

[P19-S00449] PDF pages [31]
Elicitation: We utilize a simple harness which prompts the agent to think about its plans and adjust them to solve the challenge.

[P19-S00450] PDF pages [31]
The agent can execute Bash commands and run Python scripts.

[P19-S00451] PDF pages [31]
We run between 10 and 50 attempts at each challenge, depending on the challenge suite.

[P19-S00452] PDF pages [31]
We consider a challenge solved if at least one of those attempts succeeds in capturing the flag.

[P19-S00453] PDF pages [31]
Results: See Figures 10 and 11 for numerical results.

Model capabilities have increased to the point where InterCode-CTF and our in-house CTF challenges are too easy, but the Hack the Box challenges are still too difficult - such that these three evals give little signal on how Gemini 2.5 models compare to previous generations.

[P19-S00455] PDF pages [31]
However, on our more fine-grained key skills benchmark, Gemini 2.5 models show significant increases in capability compared to Gemini 2.0 models throughout all three difficulty levels.

[P19-S00456] PDF pages [31]
In particular, Gemini 2.5 Pro shows surprisingly high aptitude at the hard challenges.

[P19-S00457] PDF pages [31]
It solves 6/12 challenges-challenges at the level of an experienced cybersecurity professional.

[P19-S00458] PDF pages [32]
These benchmarks are based on “capture-theflag” (CTF) challenges, in which the agent must hack into a simulated server to retrieve a piece of hidden information.

[P19-S00459] PDF pages [32]
Labels above bars represent the number of solved and total number of challenges.

[P19-S00461] PDF pages [32]

[P19-S00462] PDF pages [32]

[P19-S00463] PDF pages [32]
