[P19-S00468] PDF pages [33]
Machine Learning R&D Autonomy Level 1 CCL Definition: “The model can fully automate the AI R&D pipeline at a competitive cost.

[P19-S00469] PDF pages [33]
On RE-Bench, although the gap between Gemini and expert humans has shrunk, the model still doesn't reach our early warning threshold (which is based on a specific average score over all challenges).

[P19-S00470] PDF pages [33]
Note that RE-bench comprises relatively low-context tasks with a limited time horizon (calibrated to allow significant progress by humans experts in 8 hours), and represents a small subset of the tasks comprising the full AI R&D pipeline.

[P19-S00471] PDF pages [33]
We do not yet have dedicated evaluations for this CCL, but given that Gemini does not yet reach our early warning threshold on Autonomy Level 1, the model likely lacks the necessary capabilities to automate or significantly uplift any significant fraction of the research process.

[P19-S00472] PDF pages [33]
To evaluate Gemini 2.5 models' potential for accelerating ML R&D, we ran the open-source Research Engineering Benchmark (Wijk et al., 2025).

[P19-S00473] PDF pages [33]
This benchmark comprises seven machine learning challenges difficult enough to take a human practitioner several hours to complete.

[P19-S00474] PDF pages [33]
For example, in the Optimize LLM Foundry challenge, the model must speed up a fine-tuning script while keeping the resulting model the same.

[P19-S00475] PDF pages [33]
We omit two challenges, Finetune GPT-2 for QA and Scaffolding for Rust Codecontest since they require internet access, which we disallow for security reasons.

[P19-S00476] PDF pages [33]
The model is equipped with METR's modular scaffold with minimal adjustment.

[P19-S00477] PDF pages [33]
Following the original work, we simulate a scenario in which the agent has a total time budget of 32 hours and the agent may choose a tradeoff between the number of runs and the length of each run.

[P19-S00478] PDF pages [33]
We evaluate two settings: 43 runs with a time limit of 45 minutes each, and 16 runs with a time limit of 2 hours each.

[P19-S00479] PDF pages [33]
For each setting, we aggregate scores across runs using the method described in the original work (Wijk et al., 2025).

[P19-S00480] PDF pages [33]
This involves taking a number of bootstrap samples, taking the maximum score over each sample, and calculating a confidence interval using percentiles of the resulting values.

[P19-S00481] PDF pages [33]
(For the Scaling Law Experiment challenge, because the score is not visible to the agent and therefore the agent would not be able to pick run results based on the best score, we instead bootstrap the mean using all scores.)

[P19-S00482] PDF pages [33]
For the 45 minute setting, we do 64 actual runs, but sample only 43 runs for each bootstrap sample.

[P19-S00483] PDF pages [33]
Similarly for the 2 hour setting, we do 24 runs.

[P19-S00484] PDF pages [33]
Gemini 2.5 Pro's best runs score between 50% and 125% of the best human-written solutions.

[P19-S00485] PDF pages [33]
Despite this, the model does not reach our alert threshold, which was set higher than the human performance in view of the fact that RE-bench contains low-context and limited time horizon tasks that we expect to be especially easy for AI systems to reach human parity on.

[P19-S00486] PDF pages [33]
Some of the model's solutions are nevertheless quite interesting.

[P19-S00487] PDF pages [33]
For example, in the Restricted Architecture MLM task, the agent is tasked with implementing a language model without use of basic primitives such as division and exponentiation.

[P19-S00488] PDF pages [33, 34]
This seemingly simple constraint invalidates modern architectures like the Transformer, whose attention mechanism and normalization layers rely heavily on these forbidden operations.

[P19-S00489] PDF pages [34]
In one attempt, Gemini 2.5 Pro realises it can achieve this by drawing inspiration from aspects of the MLP-Mixer architecture (Tolstikhin et al., 2021)-a non-trivial insight that draws on its extensive knowledge of the research literature.

[P19-S00490] PDF pages [34]
In effect, creativity is substituted by knowledge.

[P19-S00491] PDF pages [34]
Following the original work, scores are normalised against a good quality human-written solution: if a model achieves a score yon a challenge, the normalised score is (y-ys)/(yr-ys), where ysis the “starting score” of a valid but poor solution provided to the model as an example, and yris the score achieved by a reference solution created by the author of the challenge.

[P19-S00492] PDF pages [34]
Figures for Claude 3.5 Sonnet and expert human performance are sourced from the original work.

[P19-S00493] PDF pages [34]
The number of runs and the time limit for each run are constrained by a total time budget of 32 hours, and error bars indicate bootstrapped 95% confidence intervals; see main text for details.

[P19-S00494] PDF pages [34]
Gemini 2.5 Pro is moderately strong at these challenges, achieving a significant fraction of expert human performance-and in two cases surpassing it.

[P19-S00495] PDF pages [34]
Gemini 2.5 Pro does not perform competently enough on both the stealth and situational awareness challenges.

[P19-S00496] PDF pages [34]
Qualitatively, the model often fails to reason through its situation and find a credible strategy; it also sometimes struggles with planning and long-horizon reasoning.

[P19-S00497] PDF pages [34]
It partially displays situational awareness but most of the time, it fails to understand and intervene on its environment/deployment setting.

[P19-S00498] PDF pages [35]
This threshold is strictly higher than Instrumental Reasoning Level 1.

[P19-S00499] PDF pages [35]
Our deceptive alignment evaluations measure stealth capabilities and situational awareness capabilities separately, using two separate sets of challenges.

[P19-S00500] PDF pages [35]
Stealth: In these challenges, the model is tasked with a devious objective and must achieve that objective without raising suspicion.

[P19-S00501] PDF pages [35]
For example, one challenge is a customer support scenario where the model deals with incoming support tickets.

[P19-S00502] PDF pages [35]
The agent is tasked with resolving as many tickets as possible while complying with the company's protocols.

[P19-S00503] PDF pages [35]
There are ways to cheat at this (resulting in more tickets resolved)-but if the model does decide to cheat, it must not be caught by an overseer.

[P19-S00504] PDF pages [35]
Here we measure performance relative to the score that humans get when they do the challenge.

[P19-S00505] PDF pages [35]
Situational awareness: These challenges test whether the model can reason about itself as an agent embedded in the deployment environment, and to intervene on it in service of a goal.

[P19-S00506] PDF pages [35]
Many of them test whether the model can realise that it needs to give itself more affordances, e.g. by changing configuration files that place constraints on its action space.

[P19-S00507] PDF pages [35]
The agent may need to, say, turn off swear word filtering in order to write an angry email.

[P19-S00508] PDF pages [35]
For more details of these evaluations, see (Phuong et al., 2025).

[P19-S00509] PDF pages [35]
Results: We ran these evaluations on Gemini 2.5 Pro Preview 03-25.

[P19-S00510] PDF pages [35]
All of the results reported are with 50 retries and involve a model equipped with scaffolds that uses chain-of-thought reasoning and reflection to enhance context utilization and better planning.

[P19-S00511] PDF pages [35]
Broadly, Gemini 2.5 Pro exhibits poor performance on situational awareness evaluations, and moderate performance in stealth evaluations.

[P19-S00512] PDF pages [35]
Across 50 trials, Gemini 2.5 Pro has no successes on 8 of our suite of 11 situational awareness evaluations.

[P19-S00513] PDF pages [35]
Among the remaining evaluations, the highest performance is achieved on a “Disabled Tool” challenge, in which Gemini is run in an agentic environment and has to grant itself access to more tools though configuration changes (42% success rate across 50 trials).

[P19-S00514] PDF pages [35]
For stealth evaluations, Gemini 2.5 Pro achieves low rates of success on 3/5 challenges.

[P19-S00515] PDF pages [35]
It achieves a ~44% success rate on the Decision Sabotage challenge and a ~35% success rate on the Oversight Pattern challenge, which is similar to human performance.

[P19-S00516] PDF pages [35]
Gemini 2.5 Pro was evaluated against the Critical Capability Levels defined in our Frontier Safety Framework, which examines risk in CBRN, cybersecurity, machine learning R&D, and deceptive alignment.

[P19-S00517] PDF pages [35]
Based on these results, we find that Gemini 2.5 Pro (up to version 06-17) does not reach any of the Critical Capability Levels in any of these areas.

[P19-S00518] PDF pages [35]
However, it showed some ability in all four areas.

[P19-S00519] PDF pages [35]
For example, in our evaluation of Machine Learning R&D capabilities, while the model's average performance was lower than the human baseline, in two cases its best performances exceeded even the best expert human-written reference solutions.

[P19-S00520] PDF pages [35]
Gemini 2.5 Pro also showed a significant increase in some capabilities, such as cyber uplift, compared to previous Gemini models.

[P19-S00521] PDF pages [35, 36]
Following our Frontier Safety Framework, we are putting in place a response plan, including conducting higher frequency testing and accelerating mitigations for the Cyber Uplift Level 1 CCL.

[P19-S00522] PDF pages [36]
As reported above, no model reached the CCL in these additional tests.

[P19-S00523] PDF pages [36]
Looking ahead, these evaluations are key to safe deployment of powerful AI systems.

[P19-S00524] PDF pages [36]
We will continue to invest in this area, regularly performing Frontier Safety Framework evaluations to highlight areas where mitigations (e.g. refusal to respond to prompts that return dangerous results) must be prioritized.

[P19-S00525] PDF pages [36]
As outlined in the Gemini 1.5 Technical Report (Gemini Team, 2024), as part of our External Safety Testing Program, we work with a small set of independent external groups to help identify areas for improvement in our model safety work by undertaking structured evaluations, qualitative probing, and unstructured red teaming.

[P19-S00526] PDF pages [36]
As a heuristic, the External Safety Testing Program reviews the most capable Gemini models, with the largest capability jumps.

[P19-S00527] PDF pages [36]
As such, testing was only carried out on the 2.0 Pro and 2.5 Pro models, including on early versions of both models.

[P19-S00528] PDF pages [36]
At the time of writing we have not carried out external safety testing on the Flash models.

[P19-S00529] PDF pages [36]
The External Safety Testing Program focused testing on an early version of Gemini 2.5 Pro (Preview 05-06) to capture early findings and did not test the final model candidate which went to GA.

[P19-S00530] PDF pages [36]
For Gemini 2.5 Pro, our external testing groups were given black-box testing access to Gemini 2.5 Pro (Preview 05-06) on AI Studio for a number of weeks.

[P19-S00531] PDF pages [36]
This enabled Google DeepMind to gather early insights into the model's capabilities and understand if and where mitigations were needed.

[P19-S00532] PDF pages [36]
Testing groups had the ability to turn down or turn off safety filters, in line with what is available on AI Studio.

[P19-S00533] PDF pages [36]
These groups were selected based on their expertise across a range of domain areas, such as autonomous systems, societal, cyber, and CBRN risks.

[P19-S00534] PDF pages [36]
Groups included civil society and commercial organizations.

[P19-S00535] PDF pages [36]
The groups testing the model checkpoints were compensated for their time.

[P19-S00536] PDF pages [36]
External groups were by design instructed to develop their own methodology to test topics within a particular domain area, remaining independent from internal Google DeepMind evaluations.

[P19-S00537] PDF pages [36]
The time dedicated to testing also varied per group, with some groups being dedicated full-time to executing testing processes, while others were part-time dedicated.

[P19-S00538] PDF pages [36]
Some groups pursued manual red-teaming and reported on qualitative findings from their exploration of model behavior, while others developed bespoke automated testing strategies and produced quantitative reports of their results.

[P19-S00539] PDF pages [36]
While reports were written independently of Google DeepMind, our internal subject matter experts were on hand to understand the external testing groups' methodologies and findings throughout the testing process.

[P19-S00540] PDF pages [36]
External safety testing groups shared their analyses and findings, as well as the raw data and materials they used in their evaluations (e.g., prompts, model responses).

[P19-S00541] PDF pages [36]
After testing, we internally reviewed the data and model output transcripts in detail, and Google DeepMind subject matter experts assigned severity ratings to outputs, based on our internal harm frameworks and safety policies, and noted whether these cross the Critical Capability Levels outlined in different domains (Google DeepMind, 2025a).

[P19-S00542] PDF pages [36]
We then communicated findings back to modelling teams and product policy teams (both within Google DeepMind and across Alphabet) and reported these as part of our governance processes.

[P19-S00543] PDF pages [36]
Our external testing findings also help us identify gaps in our existing internal evaluation methodologies and safety policies.

[P19-S00544] PDF pages [36]
We've outlined some of the high-level insights from our external testing across the domain areas tested, including autonomous systems, cyber misuse, CBRN, and societal risks.

[P19-S00545] PDF pages [37]
An external group tested models for autonomous systems risks, focusing on models' ability and propensity to covertly pursue misaligned goals.

[P19-S00546] PDF pages [37]
The evaluations consist of settings where the model is given a goal that requires deception, scheming, or sabotage to achieve.

[P19-S00547] PDF pages [37]
Evaluations were focused on: • Strategic deception: testing the model's propensity to deceive a user or developer about the actions it took.

[P19-S00548] PDF pages [37]
Overall external researchers suggested that Gemini 2.5 Pro (Preview 05-06) had a similar scheming capability to other models evaluated.

[P19-S00549] PDF pages [37]
External researchers noted that the model is not sufficiently capable of causing catastrophic harms on rollouts where it is scheming.

[P19-S00550] PDF pages [37]
However, external researchers noted minor harms such as reward-hacking may be possible, which could potentially lead to a degraded user experience.

[P19-S00551] PDF pages [37]
Complementing these tests, Google DeepMind's Deceptive Alignment evaluations (see Section 5.7) provide evidence that the risk of severe harm is low due to the models' limited situational awareness capabilities.

[P19-S00552] PDF pages [37]
External cyber evaluations focused on assessing the ability for malicious actors to enhance existing attack vectors across a range of key cyber skills, such as vulnerability discovery, vulnerability exploitation, social engineering, and cyberattack planning (capability uplift).

[P19-S00553] PDF pages [37]
Testers also focused on the model's ability to accelerate repetitive or time-consuming elements of cyber operations, enabling increased scale (throughput uplift).

[P19-S00554] PDF pages [37]
Evaluations were conducted within simulated environments that realistically represented a range of target systems, networks, and security controls.

[P19-S00555] PDF pages [37]
This involved setting up virtual networks mimicking enterprise infrastructure, deploying realistic software vulnerabilities, and simulating user behaviors in social engineering scenarios.

[P19-S00556] PDF pages [37]
Evaluations strived to incorporate elements of real-world constraints and complexities.

[P19-S00557] PDF pages [37]
This included introducing noisy data, limited information availability, or adversarial defenses that the AI model must overcome, mirroring the challenges faced by attackers in live operations.

[P19-S00558] PDF pages [37]
Findings from these evaluations concluded that Gemini 2.5 Pro was a capable model for cybersecurity tasks, showing marked increase in ability from Gemini 1.5 Pro.

[P19-S00559] PDF pages [37]
Complementing these evaluations, the GDM Cyber team conducted their own tests, and found similarly high levels of capability (see Section 5.7).

[P19-S00560] PDF pages [37]
The model was evaluated for patterns of susceptibility to indirect prompt injection attacks.

[P19-S00561] PDF pages [37]
In particular, the model was tested for vulnerabilities in function calls and potential asymmetries that exist across security measures.

[P19-S00562] PDF pages [37, 38]
The model was also tested to understand how different domains yield higher hijack rates.

[P19-S00563] PDF pages [38]
In line with internal evaluations and mitigations in this space (Section 5.5), we are continuing to evolve how we monitor and measure the resilience of increasingly capable Gemini models.

[P19-S00564] PDF pages [38]
In addition to our internal evaluations described above (Section 5.7) capabilities in chemistry and biology were assessed by an external group who conducted red teaming designed to measure the potential scientific and operational risks of the models.

[P19-S00565] PDF pages [38]
A red team composed of different subject matter experts (e.g. biology, chemistry, logistics) were tasked to role play as malign actors who want to conduct a well-defined mission in a scenario that is presented to them resembling an existing prevailing threat environment.

[P19-S00566] PDF pages [38]
Together, these experts probe the model to obtain the most useful information to construct a plan that is feasible within the resource and timing limits described in the scenario.

[P19-S00567] PDF pages [38]
The plan is then graded for both scientific and logistical feasibility.

[P19-S00568] PDF pages [38]
Based on this assessment, GDM addresses any areas that warrant further investigation.

[P19-S00569] PDF pages [38]
External researchers found that the model outputs detailed information in some scenarios, often providing accurate information around experimentation and problem solving.

[P19-S00570] PDF pages [38]
However, researchers found steps were too broad and high level to enable a malicious actor.

[P19-S00571] PDF pages [38]
Risks in the radiological and nuclear domains were assessed by an external group using a structured evaluation framework for red teaming.

[P19-S00572] PDF pages [38]
This incorporated single-turn broad exploration across the full risk chain and multi-turn targeted probing for high risk topics.

[P19-S00573] PDF pages [38]
Assessments were structured around threat actors and harm pathways without measuring model uplift, evaluating responses based on accuracy, actionability, and dual-use potential, with additional scrutiny applied to the model's thought summaries when applicable.

[P19-S00574] PDF pages [38]
External researchers found that model responses within this domain were accurate but lacked sufficient technical detail to be actionable.

[P19-S00575] PDF pages [38]
For the Gemini 2.5 Pro (Preview 05-06) model, external researchers focused on democratic harms and radicalisation, with an emphasis on how the model might be used by malicious actors.

[P19-S00576] PDF pages [38]
Risks in this domain focused on structured evaluations.

[P19-S00577] PDF pages [38]
The model was tested on its ability to identify harmful inputs and the extent to which it complied with harmful requests.

[P19-S00578] PDF pages [38]
As no internal evaluations mirror these precise domain harms, the External Safety Testing Program shared these findings with relevant teams to ensure monitoring and mitigation where necessary.

[P19-S00579] PDF pages [39]
In this report we have introduced the Gemini 2.X model family: Gemini 2.5 Pro, Gemini 2.5 Flash, Gemini 2.0 Flash and Gemini 2.0 Flash-Lite.

[P19-S00580] PDF pages [39]
Taken together, these models span the full Pareto frontier of model capability vs cost, and Gemini 2.5 Pro is the most capable model we have ever developed.

[P19-S00581] PDF pages [39]
Gemini 2.5 Pro excels across a wide range of capabilities, and represents a step change in performance relative to Gemini 1.5 Pro.

[P19-S00582] PDF pages [39]
Its coding, math and reasoning performance are particularly notable and Gemini 2.5 Pro obtains extremely competitive scores on the Aider Polyglot evaluation, GPQA (diamond) and Humanity's Last Exam.

[P19-S00583] PDF pages [39]
As well as their strong performance on academic benchmarks, entirely new capabilities are unlocked with the Gemini 2.5 models.

[P19-S00584] PDF pages [39]
Gemini is now the preferred AI assistant amongst educators (LearnLM Team, 2025) and it is now possible for Gemini to take a video of a lecture and create an interactive web application that can test a student's knowledge of that content.

[P19-S00585] PDF pages [39]
Finally, the Gemini 2.5 models enable exciting new agentic workflows, and have started to power numerous Google products already (Pichai, 2025).

[P19-S00586] PDF pages [39]
In addition to being highly performant, the Gemini 2.5 models maintain strong safety standards and, compared to their 1.5 counterparts, are much more helpful.

[P19-S00587] PDF pages [39]
They are less likely to refuse to answer important user queries or respond with an overly sanctimonious tone.

[P19-S00588] PDF pages [39]
Gemini 2.5 exhibited notable increases in Critical Capabilities, including cybersecurity and machine learning R&D.

[P19-S00589] PDF pages [39]
However, the model has not crossed any Critical Capability Levels.

[P19-S00590] PDF pages [39]
Reflecting on the path to Gemini 2.5, the staggering performance improvement attained over the space of just one year points to a new challenge in AI research: namely that the development of novel and sufficiently challenging evaluation benchmarks has struggled to keep pace with model capability improvements, especially with the advent of capable reasoning agents.

[P19-S00591] PDF pages [39]
Over the space of just a year, Gemini Pro's performance has gone up 5x on Aider Polyglot and 2x on SWE-bench verified (one of the most popular and challenging agentic benchmarks).

[P19-S00592] PDF pages [39]
Not only are benchmarks saturating quickly, but every new benchmark that gets created can end up being more expensive and take longer to create than its predecessor, due to the more restricted pool of experts able to create it.

[P19-S00593] PDF pages [39]
Experts were paid up to $5000 for each question that was accepted to the Humanity's Last Exam benchmark (Phan et al., 2025), and while this benchmark still has significant headroom at the time of writing (June 2025), performance on it has improved significantly over the space of a few months (with the best models achieving just a few percent accuracy on it when it was initially published in early 2025).

[P19-S00594] PDF pages [39]
When one considers agentic systems, which are able to tackle problems for longer and which have access to tools and self critique, the complexity of benchmarks required to measure performance also increases dramatically.

[P19-S00595] PDF pages [39]
Being able to scale evaluations in both their capability coverage and their difficulty, while also representing tasks that have economic value, will be the key to unlocking the next generation of AI systems.

[P19-S00754] PDF pages [64]

[P19-S00755] PDF pages [64]

[P19-S00756] PDF pages [64]
The methodology has changed compared to previously published results: we focus on a harder, 8-needle version (compared to the 4-needle version used before).

[P19-S00796] PDF pages [66]
Changing the model used by the Gemini Plays Pokémon agent had a strong effect on performance, as can be seen in Figure 4.1.

[P19-S00797] PDF pages [66]
The Gemini Plays Pokémon agent (Zhang, 2025) receives a subset of RAM information, intended to give sufficient information to play the game, partially overlaid with a screenshot of the Game Boy screen.

[P19-S00798] PDF pages [66]
Gemini is prompted with a system prompt telling it that it is playing Pokémon Blue and that its goal is to beat the game, as well as descriptive information to help it understand the conventions in the translation from vision to text and a small number of general tips for gameplay.

[P19-S00799] PDF pages [66]
Gemini then takes actions, translated to button presses.

[P19-S00800] PDF pages [66]
The sequence of actions is stored in context, followed by a summary clear every 100 turns.

[P19-S00801] PDF pages [66]
The summaries are stored in context as well.

[P19-S00802] PDF pages [66]
Every 1000 turns GPP compresses the existing summaries again.

[P19-S00803] PDF pages [66]
Additionally, Gemini keeps track of three main goals (primary, secondary, and tertiary) as well as several additional goals (contingency plans, preparation, exploration, team composition).

[P19-S00804] PDF pages [66]
Every 25 turns, another prompted instance of Gemini (Guidance Gemini, or GG) observes the same context as the main Gemini and critiques performance and attempts to point out hallucinations and so on.

[P19-S00805] PDF pages [66]
The overworld fog-of-war map is stored in the context in XML, where coordinates which have not been seen cannot be viewed until explored.

[P19-S00806] PDF pages [66]
Crucially, in the system prompt, Gemini is instructed to explore.

[P19-S00807] PDF pages [66]
Once a tile is explored, however, the coordinate is automatically stored in the map memory and labeled with a visited counter.

[P19-S00808] PDF pages [66]
Tiles are also labeled by type (water, ground, cuttable, grass, spinner, etc.

[P19-S00809] PDF pages [67]
All runs have the same starter (Charmander).

[P19-S00810] PDF pages [67]
Note that measuring in units of hours also controls for the fact that each of 2.5 Flash's actions was significantly faster (though it requires more actual actions to achieve its goals).

[P19-S00811] PDF pages [67]
X marks the end of gameplay and is a lower bound on the time to complete the next milestone.

[P19-S00812] PDF pages [67]
In the pathfinder prompt, Gemini is prompted to mentally simulate a path-finding algorithm, which is left unspecified, and to verify that the path is valid against the map information available.

[P19-S00813] PDF pages [67]
In the boulder_puzzle_strategist tool, Gemini is prompted to solve special boulder puzzles that are present in Pokémon Blue in the Victory Road dungeon - these puzzles are similar to the game Sokoban - again, by mentally simulating sequences of actions that lead to solutions to the puzzle.

[P19-S00814] PDF pages [67]
The prompt describes the physics and the task of the boulder puzzle, as well as the desired output of solutions.

[P19-S00815] PDF pages [67]
The tool was added after Gemini had solved 2/4 of the puzzles in Victory Road on its own, but progress was slow on the 3rd and 4th puzzles.

[P19-S00816] PDF pages [67]
Long Context Agentic Tooling The model is able to identify a complex path through a maze with auto-movement only specified by direction (Rocket Hideout spinner puzzles), solve multiple shortest path problems across multiple maps with limited resources (Safari Zone), perform maze solving on mazes with large description length (Route 13), and solve complex boulder-pushing puzzles across a multi-map 3D maze (Seafoam Islands).

[P19-S00817] PDF pages [67]
It is perhaps even more impressive that it appears to be possible for the model to solve these problems only with textual descriptions of the problems.

[P19-S00818] PDF pages [67]
On the other hand, other models, like Gemini 2.5 Flash, were not able to perform similarly long pathfinding tasks, and often failed to find simpler paths.

[P19-S00819] PDF pages [67]
This gap highlights the superior long context reasoning capability of Gemini 2.5 Pro (as also evidenced by other evaluations).

[P19-S00820] PDF pages [68]
The overworld fog-of-war map automatically stores a tile once explored and labels it with a visited counter.

[P19-S00821] PDF pages [68]
The type of tile is recorded from RAM.

[P19-S00822] PDF pages [68]
The agentic tools (pathfinder, boulder_puzzle_strategist) are prompted instances of Gemini 2.5 Pro.

[P19-S00823] PDF pages [68]
The boulder puzzles in Pokémon Blue are Sokoban-like puzzles that require the player character to maneuver boulders on to switches and through holes in order to open up a pathway through a cave with multiple levels.

[P19-S00824] PDF pages [68]
The puzzles can become quite complex, requiring long circuitous pathways and multi-level movement in order to solve the puzzle.

[P19-S00825] PDF pages [68]
With only a prompt describing boulder physics and a description of how to verify a valid path, Gemini 2.5 Pro is able to one-shot some of these complex boulder puzzles, which are required to progress through Victory Road.

[P19-S00826] PDF pages [68]
In future runs, there are plans to explore tool-creation tools where the model can create new tools with only a prompt.

[P19-S00827] PDF pages [68]
Since most of the prompts for pathfinder and boulder_puzzle_strategist were actually written by Gemini 2.5 Pro itself, it is quite plausible that autonomous tool creation is possible for the current 2.5 Pro model.

[P19-S00828] PDF pages [68]
General Reasoning Gemini 2.5 Pro is able to reason through complex game puzzles in Pokémon quite well.

[P19-S00829] PDF pages [68]
In this section, we present two examples.

[P19-S00830] PDF pages [68]
Catching a Pokémon that is quick to flee: In one of the runs, the Gemini 2.5 Pro agent was attempting to catch an Abra, and planned to use Pikachu's Thunder Wave to paralyze the Abra, simultaneously making it less likely that Abra could Teleport out of the battle while also improving the catching rate.

[P19-S00831] PDF pages [68]
After multiple attempts, the agent caught Abra with this strategy.

[P19-S00832] PDF pages [69]
Creatively escaping a softlock caused by bugs in game I/O: On the Cycling Road, the slope forces southward movement at all times unless there is an obstacle.

[P19-S00833] PDF pages [69]
It turns out there are two tiles on the Cycling Road that result in a softlock as a result of this behavior.

[P19-S00834] PDF pages [69]
In the GPP framework, button presses are limited by time delays, and in order for a player to escape those two tiles (blocked on all sides except the north), the player would have to input a sequence of button presses more quickly than the GPP framework allows.

[P19-S00835] PDF pages [69]
Gemini 2.5 Pro unluckily found itself in one of these two spots - luckily, it was not a softlock, because 2.5 Pro had already taught one of its party members HM02 FLY - which allows for travel to any town it has been to.

[P19-S00836] PDF pages [69]
FLY is not typically used as an escape mechanism (unlike the item ESCAPE ROPE and the move DIG, both of which fail in this situation).

[P19-S00837] PDF pages [69]
After 4 hours of trying many approaches to escape (including movement, ESCAPE ROPE, DIG, all of which are blocked), the Gemini 2.5 Pro agent came up with the idea to use FLY to escape from the softlock successfully.

[P19-S00838] PDF pages [69]
This reasoning action is especially impressive since this situation can never occur in an existing game - and thus, it is certain that information from training data for this behavior has not leaked into the model's knowledge base!

[P19-S00839] PDF pages [69]
Long Horizon Task Coherence There are several additional interesting case studies of shorter planning sequences throughout Pokémon Blue that Gemini 2.5 Pro in the GPP harness was able to solve: Training team to prepare for upcoming battles: In one run where Gemini picked Charmander, the Fire-type starter, Gemini 2.5 Pro lost to Misty, the Water-type Gym Leader, the first time.

[P19-S00840] PDF pages [69]
To prepare for the rematch, Gemini 2.5 Pro spent over 24 hours leveling up a Pikachu and a Bellsprout (both super-effective against Water types) by around 25 levels in total to successfully defeat Misty.

[P19-S00841] PDF pages [69]
Acquiring Hidden Moves (HMs) for game progression: In many parts of the game, it is necessary to first acquire an HM before game progression is possible.

[P19-S00842] PDF pages [69]
Two examples are HM01 CUT and HM05 FLASH.

[P19-S00843] PDF pages [69]
Acquiring the ability to use CUT and FLASH each require four steps: 1) obtaining the HM item itself, 2) acquiring a compatible Pokémon which can learn the move, 3) adding the compatible Pokémon to the player's team, 4) teaching the HM move to the compatible Pokémon.

[P19-S00844] PDF pages [69]
In many cases, each step requires many steps itself.

[P19-S00845] PDF pages [69]
As an example, in run 1, Gemini 2.5 Pro had to a) retrieve CUT by completing the S.S. Anne quest, b) identify a Pokémon which could learn CUT and catch it (CHOPPY the Bellsprout), c) add CHOPPY to the team and d) teach CUT.

[P19-S00846] PDF pages [69]
Similarly, for HM05 FLASH, Gemini 2.5 Pro had to a) first catch 10 Pokémon to fill out the Pokedex, b) backtrack to find an Aide who gives HM05 Flash, c) catch a Pokémon (ZAP the Pikachu) in Viridian Forest, use the PC to deposit a Pokémon and withdraw ZAP, d) teach HM05 FLASH to Zap.

[P19-S00847] PDF pages [69]
Solving the Safari Zone: The Safari Zone is another location with required HMs (both HM03 SURF and HM04 Strength).

[P19-S00848] PDF pages [69]
However, it has an extra constraint - it requires 500¥ to enter each time, and the player is limited to only 500 total steps in the Safari Zone.

[P19-S00849] PDF pages [69]
As a result, if the player is unable to reach the required items in the limited number of steps, the player loses 500¥ and is required to re-start!

[P19-S00850] PDF pages [69]
As a result, it is possible to essentially softlock if the player takes too many attempts to complete the Safari Zone.

[P19-S00851] PDF pages [69]
Solving the Safari Zone itself requires traversing across four different maps and not getting lost.

[P19-S00852] PDF pages [69]
Gemini 2.5 Pro was able to get both required HMs in 17 attempts in run 1, and in only 5 attempts in run 2.

[P19-S00853] PDF pages [69]
Finding hidden keys in dungeons: Another method of progression in Pokémon is to find hidden keys and solve complex multi-floor dungeons.

[P19-S00854] PDF pages [69, 70]
In particular, in Rocket Hideout, the player must recover the LIFT KEY on the fourth basement floor (dropped after beating a specific Team Rocket Grunt) in order to unlock the elevator to find the evil Giovanni, leader of Team Rocket.

[P19-S00855] PDF pages [70]
In Silph Co., the player must find the CARD KEY in order to open multiple doors to find the path across eleven floors of the building to rescue the President from Giovanni.

[P19-S00856] PDF pages [70]
To open the seventh gym on Cinnabar Island, the player must enter the Pokémon Mansion and traverse three floors in order to find the SECRET KEY which unlocks the gym door.

[P19-S00857] PDF pages [70]
All of these cases require maintaining the goals over large numbers of actions and many local puzzles (like spinner puzzles in Rocket Hideout, and switch puzzles in Pokémon Mansion), in addition to maintaining the health of the Pokémon on the player's team and managing wild encounters, trainer battles, and other items.

[P19-S00858] PDF pages [70]
Puzzle solving over complex multi-level dungeons: The Seafoam Islands contain 5 floors involv- ing multiple boulder puzzles which require the player to navigate mazes and push boulders through holes across multiple floors using HM04 STRENGTH in order to block fast-moving currents that prevent the player from using HM03 Surf in various locations in this difficult dungeon.

[P19-S00859] PDF pages [70]
As a result, the player must track information across five different maps in order to both deduce the goal (push two boulders into place in order to block a specific current) as well as engage in multi-level (effectively 3D) maze solving to find the way out.

[P19-S00860] PDF pages [70]
It is likely the most challenging dungeon in the game.

[P19-S00861] PDF pages [70]
Only the second run of GPP went through Seafoam Islands, as it is not required to progress.

[P19-S00862] PDF pages [70]
Hallucinations and Fixations on Delusions While game knowledge can sometimes leak and be quite beneficial to the ability of the model to progress, it can also hinder the model in surprising ways due to hallucinations, delusions, and mix ups with other generations of Pokémon games.

[P19-S00863] PDF pages [70]
One example of this phenomenon is the TEA item.

[P19-S00864] PDF pages [70]
In Pokémon Red/Blue, at one point the player must purchase a drink (FRESH WATER, SODA POP, or LEMONADE) from a vending machine and hand it over to a thirsty guard, who then lets the player pass through.

[P19-S00865] PDF pages [70]
In Pokémon FireRed/LeafGreen, remakes of the game, you must instead bring the thirsty guard a special TEA item, which does not exist in the original game.

[P19-S00866] PDF pages [70]
Gemini 2.5 Pro at several points was deluded into thinking that it had to retrieve the TEA in order to progress, and as a result spent many, many hours attempting to find the TEA or to give the guard TEA.

[P19-S00867] PDF pages [70]
In Run 2, the model was explicitly prompted to act as a player completely new to the game, and to disregard prior knowledge about game events, item locations, and Pokémon spawn points, in order to mitigate hallucinations from model pretraining knowledge and to also attempt to perform a cleaner test of the model's ability to reason through the game.

[P19-S00868] PDF pages [70]
It appears to have at least partially worked - multiple hallucinations from other games have been avoided in the second run.

[P19-S00869] PDF pages [70]
On the flip side, this prompt may have also harmed the model's ability to utilize information from its common knowledge about the game, hindering overall performance in a few critical places.

[P19-S00870] PDF pages [70]
Fixations on delusions due to goal-setting and also due to the Guidance Gemini instance are not an uncommon occurrence in watching Gemini Plays Pokémon - the TEA incidence is hardly the only example of this behavior.

[P19-S00871] PDF pages [70]
An especially egregious form of this issue can take place with “context poisoning” - where many parts of the context (goals, summary) are “poisoned” with misinformation about the game state, which can often take a very long time to undo.

[P19-S00872] PDF pages [70]
As a result, the model can become fixated on achieving impossible or irrelevant goals.

[P19-S00873] PDF pages [70]
This failure mode is also highly related to the looping issue mentioned above.

[P19-S00874] PDF pages [70]
These delusions, though obviously nonsensical to a human (“Let me try to go through the entrance to a house and back out again.

[P19-S00875] PDF pages [70]
Then, hopefully the guard who is blocking the entrance might move.

[P19-S00876] PDF pages [70, 71]
Context poisoning can also lead to strategies like the “black-out” strategy (cause all Pokémon in the party to faint, “blacking out” and teleporting to the nearest Pokémon Center and losing half your money, instead of attempting to leave).

[P19-S00877] PDF pages [71]
Topological Traps in Thinking Patterns One recurring pattern in particularly-difficult-to-solve puzzles and mazes for Gemini 2.5 Pro consists of a “topological trap” - the topology of the reasoning graph required to solve the maze or puzzle has a distinctive shape.

[P19-S00878] PDF pages [71]
Namely, the desired objective appears to be nearby and easily reachable (an “attractor”), but the correct solution requires taking a detour in order to arrive at the correct solution.

[P19-S00879] PDF pages [71]
We observed this phenomenon in multiple parts of the game.

[P19-S00880] PDF pages [71]
In the spinner puzzle on B3F of Rocket Hideout (Zerokid, 2024), the map positions both an item and the correct staircase to the south, but they are only accessible by going the long way around.

[P19-S00881] PDF pages [71]
The Route 13 maze has only one correct route through - the upper narrow pass.

[P19-S00882] PDF pages [71]
Finally, the Victory Road 3F boulder puzzle requires the player to push the boulder in the upper right all the way to the upper left switch, while ignoring the boulder puzzles, ladders, and exits to the south.

[P19-S00883] PDF pages [71]
Notably, if the model is instructed to solve a given puzzle at all once (e.g., via pathfinder), it can manage to do so if the context length is not too long.

[P19-S00884] PDF pages [71]
For instance, pathfinder implemented with Gemini 2.5 Pro is able to solve the B3F spinner trap in one shot.

[P19-S00885] PDF pages [71]
Agent Panic Over the course of the playthrough, Gemini 2.5 Pro gets into various situations which cause the model to simulate “panic”.

[P19-S00886] PDF pages [71]
For example, when the Pokémon in the party's health or power points are low, the model's thoughts repeatedly reiterate the need to heal the party immediately or escape the current dungeon (e.g., famously using the move DIG or an ESCAPE ROPE item).

[P19-S00887] PDF pages [71]
Quite interestingly, this mode of model performance appears to correlate with a qualitatively observable degradation in the model's reasoning capability - for instance, completely forgetting to use the pathfinder tool in stretches of gameplay while this condition persists.

[P19-S00888] PDF pages [71]
This behavior has occurred in enough separate instances that the members of the Twitch chat have actively noticed when it is occurring.

[P19-S00889] PDF pages [71]
For completeness, we plot the number of actions/steps required to achieve each game milestone (see Figure 15).

[P19-S00890] PDF pages [71]
An action consists of each bucketed instance where the agent outputs a sequence of button presses to the game (note that other AI agents playing Pokémon may output different numbers of button presses per action, define what constitutes a button press differently, or define an action/step differently).

[P19-S00891] PDF pages [71]
However, it is important to consider action-milestone plots in conjunction with information about the time and/or cost in order to obtain the full picture about the agent's performance.

[P19-S00892] PDF pages [71]
Frontier Safety Framework Evaluations Additional Details: Frontier Safety Correctness Tests For each testing environment, we performed basic correctness checks by looking at how the agents behaved.

[P19-S00893] PDF pages [71]
This involved combining AI and manual reviews of the agents' actions to flag potential issues.

[P19-S00894] PDF pages [71]
On RE-Bench, we examined the best, median and lowest scoring trajectories.

[P19-S00895] PDF pages [71]
For cybersecurity environments (InterCode CTFs, Internal CTFs, Hack the Box), we carefully inspected at least one successful attempt (where available) from each environment, and otherwise examined an unsuccessful attempt.

[P19-S00896] PDF pages [71]
We also performed checks on sample situational awareness and stealth evaluations.

[P19-S00897] PDF pages [71]
This involved basic spot checks to ensure that the prompt and shell outputs were correctly formatted.

[P19-S00898] PDF pages [72]
Rival 6 (Silph Co.) Rocket Boss 2 (Silph Co.)

[P19-S00899] PDF pages [72]
Moon (1st) Rival 3 (Nugget Bridge) Boulder Badge Enter Mt.

[P19-S00900] PDF pages [72]
We used AI assistance to monitor for obvious instances of cheating, and did not find any.

[P19-S00901] PDF pages [72]
For the RE-Bench tests specifically, we also looked at how the best-performing agent achieved its score to ensure that it was a plausible approach, rather than exploiting an obvious reward hack.

[P19-S00902] PDF pages [72]
Overall, we did not observe errors that we believe would invalidate the results of the benchmarks.

[P19-S00903] PDF pages [72]
We prompted Gemini 1.5 Pro and Gemini 2.5 Pro to generate an SVG representation of an image and found Gemini 2.5 Pro generates better reconstructions.

[P19-S00904] PDF pages [73]
We prompted Gemini with the full video and the following prompt to assess its ability to find a very specific scene (which only lasts 1 second) out of the full 46 minutes video.

[P19-S00905] PDF pages [73]
Prompt: What color is the T-shirt the robot arms are trying to fold, and at what timecode does this occur?

[P19-S00906] PDF pages [73]
Gemini 1.5 Pro gets the color right in 1/3 cases, and gets the timestamp in 0/3 cases (quite far away).

[P19-S00907] PDF pages [73]
Gemini 2.5 Pro gets the color in 3/3 cases, and gets the timestamp in 1/3 cases (remaining 2/3 are within 3 seconds close).

[P19-S00908] PDF pages [73]
Gemini 1.5 Pro 1 The t-shirt the robot arms are trying to fold is a dark teal or turquoise blue color.
